sequence_number INT NOT NULL with UNIQUE (workorder_id, sequence_number) so the customer-facing "CO #1, CO #2" numbering can never collide on race; title VARCHAR(180) NOT NULL because every CO needs a one-line label the customer reads on the printed approval slip; description TEXT nullable for the longer "what + why" prose; reason_code with a five-value vocabulary (`discovered_condition`|`customer_request`|`parts_unavailable`|`scope_clarification`|`other`) so reporting can later answer "what fraction of COs come from techs discovering hidden problems vs. customers asking for upsells" without parsing free text; status with the five-state lifecycle (`draft`|`pending_approval`|`approved`|`rejected`|`cancelled`) covering compose → submit → outcome; subtotal_cents + tax_cents + total_cents stored as integers because dollar math in floats is a known way to leak pennies — service recomputes subtotal from items and total = subtotal + tax on every item write so the persisted columns can be trusted by downstream invoice rollup without re-summing; requested_by_user_id (the tech who composed it) + approved_by_user_id (the manager who stamped it) both nullable + FK SET NULL guarded on `users` so deleting a staff member doesn't blow away the audit trail of who authored/authorized what; lifecycle timestamps requested_at + approved_at + rejected_at + cancelled_at + applied_at all individually nullable so the audit trail can show "approved at 14:32 on 2026-04-24" without inventing fake timestamps for states the CO never reached — applied_at stamps with approval today and is reserved for future "approved CO totals were merged into the WO's billing rollup" semantics where apply may lag approval; approval_method with a four-value vocabulary (`verbal_signed_off`|`signed_inline`|`portal_approval`|`email`) so the printable CO PDF's signature block can pick the right wording (verbal needs "Customer verbally authorized at 2:15pm — confirmed by tech: J. Smith"; inline needs the actual signature image; portal needs "Approved via customer portal at <timestamp>"; email needs "Approved by reply to <subject>"); rejection_reason TEXT nullable but service-required when transitioning to rejected so a customer's "no thanks" always carries documentation rather than vanishing; customer_signature_name VARCHAR(160) nullable but service-required for inline + portal approval methods so the printed CO has an attribution name to display; notes TEXT for free-form internal commentary; timestamps; three indexes — workorder_id + sequence_number for the per-WO list page (always sorted by sequence), status for the cross-WO "all pending COs awaiting approval" dashboard widget, and a composite index on (workorder_id, status) for the per-WO summarize roll-up that buckets approved vs pending. `workorder_change_order_items` (change_order_id INT NOT NULL with FK CASCADE so deleting a CO sweeps its items, guarded on `workorder_change_orders` existing; kind ENUM-shape vocabulary `labor`|`part`|`fee`|`discount` so reporting + downstream invoice apply logic can attribute totals to the right WO buckets — labor items roll into labor totals, parts into parts totals, fees into misc, and discounts net out; description VARCHAR(255) NOT NULL because every line item has a customer-readable label; quantity DECIMAL(10,2) so we get fractional hours without float drift; unit_price_cents INT signed so a discount kind can carry a negative price (no separate sign column — the kind + sign on price together tell the full story); line_total_cents INT signed = round(quantity * unit_price_cents) so the per-line total is recomputed and persisted by the service on every item write rather than computed at read time; sort_order INT for explicit display ordering — the customer's CO might naturally want labor items first then parts then a discount line, and a tech-controlled sort_order is the simplest way to express that without inventing implicit ordering rules). Two typed `BaseModel` subclasses — `WorkorderChangeOrder` carrying STATUSES const for vocabulary validation, an `ALLOWED_TRANSITIONS` const map (`pending_approval` ← draft only; `approved` ← pending_approval only; `rejected` ← pending_approval only; `cancelled` ← draft OR pending_approval) — declarative state machine so illegal jumps (cancelled → approved, draft → approved, approved → rejected) are rejected at validation rather than producing nonsensical lifecycle audit trails, REASONS const + APPROVAL_METHODS const so the same vocabularies are single-sourced from the model rather than scattered through validation paths, and an `items` array property auto-hydrated lazily by the service so a single getChangeOrder round-trip returns the full graph; `WorkorderChangeOrderItem` carrying KINDS const so the kind vocabulary is single-sourced. `ChangeOrderRepository` follows the COLUMNS-const + private-hydrate pattern with two logical surfaces (change orders + items) sharing one PDO connection; `nextSequenceNumber(workorderId)` computes COALESCE(MAX(sequence_number), 0) + 1 in a single indexed query so the service can stamp a stable CO # at create time without round-tripping the row count — concurrent creates on the same WO are rare in practice, and the UNIQUE (workorder_id, sequence_number) index backstops races by failing the second insert loudly so the operator sees the conflict rather than silently overwriting; `listForWorkorder` returns all COs for a WO sorted by sequence_number ASC + id ASC (sequence is the primary sort, id breaks ties if two COs ever land on the same sequence — defensive for the race case); `castColumn` + `castItemColumn` are narrow casting helpers that collapse empty-string inputs to NULL on nullable columns + cast cents fields to int + quantity to float so blank form submissions don't leave artifact whitespace and don't trip type-juggling bugs at the SQL boundary. `ChangeOrderService` orchestrates the gates + validation + state machine + auto-recompute totals: three permission gates (`workorder_change_orders.view` for read paths, `workorder_change_orders.manage` for write paths covering create/edit/items/submit/cancel, and `workorder_change_orders.approve` for approve + reject — separation of duties matching the invoice voiding pattern elsewhere in the codebase, so a tech with manage can compose drafts and submit for approval but cannot stamp their own CO as customer-approved; managers + admin via `*` get all three; dispatcher gets view + manage; technician gets view + manage; accounting gets view-only for invoice reconciliation). `createChangeOrder` validates required title + reason_code vocabulary, stamps requested_by_user_id from the actor, auto-assigns the sequence number via repo, forces status='draft' and zero totals on creation regardless of payload (the only way to skip draft is to immediately submit which goes through the lifecycle path that records requested_at). `updateChangeOrder` is the metadata mutation path that **explicitly strips** status + all lifecycle timestamps + approved_by_user_id + approval_method + rejection_reason + customer_signature_name + subtotal_cents + total_cents from the input payload — these fields can only be moved through the dedicated lifecycle methods or recomputeTotals so the service stays the single writer of those columns; updateChangeOrder rejects edits when status is not draft|pending_approval (so an approved CO becomes immutable, preventing post-hoc rewriting of what the customer authorized); when tax_cents changes, recomputeTotals fires so the persisted total_cents stays coherent. `addItem` + `updateItem` + `deleteItem` all gate on assertEditable (rejecting writes once the CO is approved/rejected/cancelled), validate kind vocabulary + positive quantity + non-negative unit_price_cents (except for discount kind which is allowed negative — discount is the explicit signed-price path), recompute the line_total at write time as round(quantity * unit_price_cents), and call recomputeTotals() afterward so subtotal_cents + total_cents are always coherent with the items + tax. `submitForApproval` rejects empty COs (cannot submit a CO with zero line items — the customer needs something to approve), draft → pending_approval, stamps requested_at; `approve` requires `.approve` gate, validates approval_method vocabulary, requires customer_signature_name when method is signed_inline OR portal_approval (because the printable PDF's signature block needs an attribution name for those methods specifically — verbal_signed_off and email both have inherent attribution: the tech who recorded the verbal yes, the email From address), pending → approved, stamps approved_at + approved_by_user_id + approval_method + customer_signature_name + applied_at; `reject` requires `.approve` gate (rejection is an authority decision matching approval, not just a manage action), requires non-blank reason, pending → rejected, stamps rejected_at + rejection_reason; `cancel` requires `.manage` only (cancelling your own draft is a routine manage action, not an authority gate), draft|pending → cancelled, stamps cancelled_at. `summarizeForWorkorder` is the invoice-rollup feeder: sums approved CO subtotal/total/count and pending CO subtotal/total/count into separate buckets so the WO billing UI can show "$X (WO base) + $Y (approved COs) = $T billable, with $Z pending in COs awaiting customer approval" without the caller re-summing — buckets are explicitly NOT a single combined total because the UI needs to distinguish "money I can already invoice for" from "money I can't bill until the customer signs off". The `?DateTimeImmutable $now` params on submit/approve/reject/cancel are for tests — production calls leave them null and let the service generate `new DateTimeImmutable()` at transition time; tests pass deterministic timestamps so the assertions can check exact stamp values. `ChangeOrderController` is the thin HTTP facade with thirteen methods covering per-WO list/create/summary, single-CO CRUD, item add/update/delete, and the four lifecycle transitions; `serializeWithItems` is the response-shape helper that nests items under data.items so a single GET returns the entire CO graph in one round-trip without forcing the UI to make a second call for line items; `reject` validates the rejection_reason in the request body and raises InvalidArgumentException when blank or absent so the route layer's error middleware can emit a 400 instead of leaking a generic 500. New module wired via `routes/modules/workorder_change_orders.php` and registered in `routes/api.php`'s loader array (after `subcontractors`). Fourteen routes total: `GET /api/workorders/{id}/change-orders` (per-WO list — workorder id in path so URL-keyed authorization can be added later without payload changes), `POST /api/workorders/{id}/change-orders`, `GET /api/workorders/{id}/change-orders/summary` (the rollup-buckets endpoint feeding the invoice UI), `GET/PUT/DELETE /api/workorder-change-orders/{id}` (single-CO CRUD using the resource id rather than nested under WO so a deep link to a specific CO doesn't need the WO id), `POST /api/workorder-change-orders/{id}/items` (add item under a CO), `PUT/DELETE /api/workorder-change-order-items/{id}` (single-item update/delete using the item id directly), and four lifecycle endpoints `POST /api/workorder-change-orders/{id}/submit`, `/approve`, `/reject`, `/cancel`. Permission additions to `config/auth.php`: `workorder_change_orders.view` + `workorder_change_orders.manage` added to manager (full owner — managers compose, edit, submit, AND approve/reject); both added to dispatcher (read for routing/visibility + manage so dispatchers can compose CO drafts when scheduling discovers extra work — approval still gated separately); both added to technician (techs are the primary CO authors — they discover extra work in the bay and need to compose drafts on the spot — but cannot approve their own work, hence no `.approve`); `workorder_change_orders.approve` added to manager only (separation of duties — admin gets it via `*`, but no other role can stamp customer approval); `workorder_change_orders.view` added to accounting (read-only for invoicing reconciliation since approved CO totals roll into the WO bill). Test coverage: 36-case `ChangeOrderServiceTest` (SQLite-gated, consistent with prior phases) — model status const vocabulary + ALLOWED_TRANSITIONS map shape + item KINDS const, sequence numbering increments per WO (1→2→3) + is independent across WOs (WO100 #1 + WO101 #1 + WO100 #2 each get the right number) + does NOT gap-fill after delete (next sequence is MAX+1, so customer-facing numbering stays monotonic even when drafts are deleted), service validation gates (title required on create, unknown reason_code rejected, requested_by_user_id stamped from actor, draft + zero totals forced on creation), item add recomputes subtotal + total (2 * 5000 → 10000 with no tax → total == subtotal), tax_cents on the CO is included in the recomputed total (subtotal 10000 + tax 825 → total 10825), zero-quantity items rejected, unknown item kind rejected, negative unit_price rejected for non-discount items, negative unit_price ALLOWED for discount kind (discount nets out: 10000 - 1000 → 9000 subtotal), updateItem recomputes line_total + CO totals coherently, deleteItem recomputes CO totals back to zero, submit rejects empty COs (no items → cannot submit), submit moves draft → pending and stamps requested_at, submit rejects already-pending CO, approve only from pending (approve a draft → reject), approve with default method (verbal_signed_off) succeeds and stamps approved_at + approved_by + applied_at, approve rejects unknown method, approve_inline_requires_signature_name (signed_inline without name → reject; with name → succeed), approve_portal_requires_signature_name (same enforcement for portal_approval), reject requires non-blank reason (whitespace → reject), reject moves pending → rejected with reason + rejected_at stamped, reject rejects already-approved CO, cancel allowed from draft, cancel allowed from pending, cancel rejected from approved, cannot add item to approved CO (assertEditable enforced), cannot update CO after approval (status check rejects), delete only allowed in draft (submitted CO cannot be deleted), update strips status + all lifecycle fields from payload (verified by trying to set status=approved + approved_at + approval_method + subtotal_cents through update — all stripped, only description survives), update tax_cents recomputes total (subtotal 10000 + new tax 825 → total 10825), summarize buckets approved + pending separately and explicitly excludes draft COs (approved 10000+800tax=10800, pending 6000, draft NOT counted), view permission gate denies listForWorkorder, manage permission gate denies createChangeOrder, **separation of duties verified** — approve permission denied while manage permitted still rejects approve (manager-can-compose-but-not-approve scenario from a tech holding only manage works), reject also requires approve permission separately, controller create returns data envelope with empty items array, controller get includes nested items (1 item with line_total 7500), controller reject requires reason in body (raises InvalidArgumentException when omitted), controller summary returns approved bucket with correct count + total. All seven new/modified files lint green via `php -l`; autoload regenerated successfully (929 → 934 classes — five new: WorkorderChangeOrder, WorkorderChangeOrderItem, ChangeOrderRepository, ChangeOrderService, ChangeOrderController; all loadable via the project autoloader).)_
- [x] Additional-tech request on WO _(10.3 — migration 142 lays down two idempotent tables: `workorder_tech_requests` (workorder_id INT NOT NULL with FK CASCADE so deleting the parent WO sweeps its open requests automatically — guarded on the `workorders` table existing so the migration runs cleanly on a partially-set-up DB; requested_by_user_id INT NOT NULL with FK RESTRICT on `users` because the requestor IS the audit trail — silently nulling the requestor would erase "who said they needed help" which is the entire point of the workflow, RESTRICT forces operator resolution before a user delete; request_type with a four-value vocabulary (`extra_hands`|`specialty`|`second_opinion`|`training`) so dispatch reporting can later distinguish "tech needs an extra body to lift the transmission" from "tech needs a specialist who actually knows refrigerant systems" from "tech wants a second opinion before condemning the head gasket" from "tech is mentoring an apprentice who needs supervised time on this job" — those four reasons drive different staffing decisions and a free-text "reason" column would have made that aggregation impossible; urgency with a four-value vocabulary (`low`|`normal`|`high`|`urgent`) so the dispatch board's "incoming requests" widget can sort the urgent ones to the top — explicitly NOT a free-form priority field because dispatchers need a fixed ladder for triage and an open input would invite "P1!!!" / "URGENT URGENT" noise; status with the five-state lifecycle (`pending`|`approved`|`declined`|`cancelled`|`fulfilled`) covering file → triage → outcome — the approved/fulfilled split is intentional because dispatch reality is "yes you can have help" often happens hours before "and here's exactly WHO can help you" — the approval is the policy decision and fulfilment is the staffing decision and conflating them would force dispatch to either hold approvals until a body is free (creating artificial backlogs) or pre-commit to a body before knowing who's actually available; estimated_hours DECIMAL(6,2) nullable so the requestor can communicate "I think this is a 4-hour ask" without forcing the dispatcher to guess; specialty_required VARCHAR(160) nullable for the free-text specialty when request_type='specialty' (e.g. "EPA-certified refrigerant tech"); reason TEXT NOT NULL because every help request must carry a "why" — anonymous "I need help" requests are not actionable; rejection_reason TEXT nullable but service-required when transitioning to declined so a refusal always carries documentation rather than vanishing into the audit trail as an unexplained no; approved_by_user_id + declined_by_user_id + cancelled_by_user_id + fulfilled_by_user_id + fulfilled_with_user_id all nullable + FK SET NULL guarded on `users` so deleting a staff member doesn't blow away the audit of who-did-what (fulfilled_with_user_id is the user who got assigned via the fulfilment, distinct from fulfilled_by_user_id who is the dispatcher who pressed the button — both are needed for accountability); lifecycle timestamps requested_at + approved_at + declined_at + cancelled_at + fulfilled_at all individually nullable so the audit trail can show "approved at 14:32, fulfilled at 15:47" without inventing fake timestamps for states the request never reached; notes TEXT for free-form internal commentary; timestamps; three indexes — workorder_id for the per-WO list page (techs and dispatchers both want "show me all open help requests for this job"), status for the cross-WO "all pending requests awaiting dispatch decision" widget, and requested_by_user_id for "show me all requests I've filed" on the tech's own dashboard. `workorder_additional_techs` (workorder_id INT NOT NULL CASCADE guarded on `workorders` existing; user_id INT NOT NULL with FK RESTRICT on `users` because deleting an active assigned tech mid-job needs to fail loudly so dispatch can reassign rather than silently orphaning labor history; tech_request_id nullable + FK SET NULL guarded on `workorder_tech_requests` so a direct dispatch-add path that doesn't originate from a request still works, and deleting a fulfilled request doesn't blow away the assignment record that came out of it; role with a four-value vocabulary (`secondary_tech`|`specialist`|`shadow`|`apprentice`) — these roles map onto labor-billing semantics (secondary_tech bills as labor, shadow does not bill, apprentice bills at apprentice rate, specialist bills at specialty rate) so the time-tracking layer downstream can pick the right rate without re-deriving role from request_type; assigned_at + assigned_by_user_id (who dispatched them) for the assignment audit, plus removed_at + removed_by_user_id + removal_reason for the soft-removal pattern — explicitly soft-removal because a tech who worked 3 hours on the WO and was then pulled to an emergency call still has 3 hours of labor that needs to bill against this WO, and a hard DELETE would orphan those time-tracking records; notes TEXT for free-form commentary; timestamps; three indexes — workorder_id for "show me everyone working this WO", a composite (workorder_id, removed_at) index because the hot read pattern is "show me ACTIVE additional techs on this WO" which filters by removed_at IS NULL, and user_id for "show me every WO I've worked on" on the tech's own dashboard. Two typed `BaseModel` subclasses — `WorkorderTechRequest` carrying STATUSES const for vocabulary validation, REQUEST_TYPES + URGENCIES consts so those vocabularies are single-sourced from the model rather than scattered through validation paths, and an `ALLOWED_TRANSITIONS` const map (`approved` ← pending only; `declined` ← pending only; `cancelled` ← pending OR approved; `fulfilled` ← approved only) — declarative state machine so illegal jumps (declined → fulfilled, fulfilled → approved, cancelled → fulfilled) get rejected at validation rather than producing nonsensical lifecycle audit trails; the "fulfilled ← approved only" rule is the load-bearing one because it forces the policy decision (approval) to precede the staffing decision (fulfilment) and prevents the shortcut of dispatch silently assigning a body without an explicit yes/no on whether the request was warranted; `WorkorderAdditionalTech` carrying ROLES const + an `isActive()` helper that returns true when removed_at === null, used everywhere the read paths need to filter active vs. historical assignments without re-implementing the rule. `TechRequestRepository` follows the COLUMNS-const + private-hydrate pattern with two logical surfaces (requests + additional techs) sharing one PDO connection; `findActiveTech(workorderId, userId)` is the UNIQUE-active enforcement helper — used by addTech and fulfilRequest before insert to reject "this user is already actively on this WO" without relying on a UNIQUE DB index (the index would block the soft-removal/re-add scenario where a user was removed and is being re-added later, which is a valid business operation); `listTechsForWorkorder` accepts an `$activeOnly` flag so the controller's listTechsForWorkorder can default to "show only active assignments" while still letting the audit/history view pull the full list including removals; `castRequestColumn` + `castTechColumn` are narrow casting helpers that collapse empty-string inputs to NULL on nullable columns, cast estimated_hours to float, cast user_id/workorder_id/tech_request_id to int so blank form submissions don't leave artifact whitespace and don't trip type-juggling bugs at the SQL boundary. `TechRequestService` orchestrates the gates + validation + state machine + soft-removal: three permission gates (`workorder_tech_requests.view` for read paths, `workorder_tech_requests.create` for filing a new request, and `workorder_tech_requests.manage` for the dispatch decisions — approve/decline/fulfil + add+remove direct techs — separation of duties matching the change-order pattern from 10.2, so a tech with view+create can ASK for help but cannot stamp their own request as approved or unilaterally pull a coworker onto the job; managers + admin via `*` get all three; dispatcher gets all three because dispatch is the operational owner of the request workflow; technician gets view + create because techs are the primary requestors but cannot make dispatch decisions on their own requests; accounting gets view-only for labor-cost reconciliation since fulfilled requests result in additional billable time on the WO). `createRequest` validates required reason text + request_type vocabulary + urgency vocabulary + positive estimated_hours when provided, stamps requested_by_user_id from the actor, forces status='pending' and requested_at=now on creation regardless of payload (the only way to skip pending is via a lifecycle transition that records the corresponding timestamp). `updateRequest` is the metadata mutation path that **explicitly strips** status + all lifecycle timestamps + approved_by/declined_by/cancelled_by/fulfilled_by/fulfilled_with from the input payload — these fields can only be moved through the dedicated lifecycle methods so the service stays the single writer of those columns; updateRequest also rejects edits when status is not pending (so an approved or declined request becomes immutable, preventing post-hoc rewriting of what was originally asked for and how dispatch responded). `approveRequest` requires `.manage` gate, validates pending → approved transition via assertTransitionAllowed, stamps approved_at + approved_by_user_id; `declineRequest` requires `.manage` gate, requires non-blank rejection_reason (a refusal with no reason is operationally useless — the requestor needs to know why so they can adjust), pending → declined, stamps declined_at + declined_by_user_id + rejection_reason; `cancelRequest` is the owner-friendly path — checks `requested_by_user_id === actor->id` and if true allows cancel WITHOUT requiring the manage gate (cancelling your OWN request is a routine operation, not a dispatch decision), only when the actor is NOT the original requestor does cancelRequest fall through to the gate.assert(.manage) check so a dispatcher cancelling someone else's request still goes through the authorization layer; pending|approved → cancelled, stamps cancelled_at + cancelled_by_user_id (so audit can show "cancelled by self" vs "cancelled by dispatcher"); `fulfilRequest` requires `.manage` gate and `user_id` in payload (dispatch must explicitly name who's being assigned — no implicit "the next available tech" magic), validates approved → fulfilled transition (cannot fulfil a pending request — must approve first, deliberate two-step), explicitly rejects assigning the same user_id who originally filed the request (you can't assign yourself as your own additional tech — you're already on the WO as the primary), consults findActiveTech to reject UNIQUE-active violations (the same user can't be assigned twice as active simultaneously — re-add allowed only after a prior removal), then within a single logical operation stamps the request fulfilled (status + fulfilled_at + fulfilled_by_user_id + fulfilled_with_user_id) AND auto-creates the workorder_additional_techs row with assigned_at + assigned_by_user_id + tech_request_id linkage so the assignment provenance traces back to the original request — this auto-creation is what makes fulfilment a single atomic dispatch action rather than two separate API calls. `addTech` is the direct add path for "a manager just walked into the bay and decided to throw an extra hand at this job without an existing request" — same UNIQUE-active enforcement, requires .manage gate, requires user_id, defaults role to secondary_tech if not specified, stamps assigned_at + assigned_by_user_id but leaves tech_request_id null (since there's no originating request); `updateTech` is the metadata mutation path that **explicitly strips** removed_at + removed_by_user_id + removal_reason from the payload — those fields can only move through removeTech so the service stays the single writer of removal state, and updateTech also rejects edits to already-removed assignments (a removed assignment is historical and immutable); `removeTech` is the soft-removal path, requires .manage gate, stamps removed_at + removed_by_user_id + optional removal_reason but does NOT delete the row so the labor-tracking history survives — a tech who worked 3 hours then got pulled still has those 3 hours billable against this WO. The `?DateTimeImmutable $now` params on all lifecycle methods are for tests — production calls leave them null and let the service generate `new DateTimeImmutable()` at transition time; tests pass deterministic timestamps so the assertions can check exact stamp values. `assertTransitionAllowed(from, to)` consults ALLOWED_TRANSITIONS[to] and rejects if `from` is not in the allowed-from list — single declarative source of truth for what moves are legal so adding a new state in the future is one map edit rather than scattered if/else throughout the lifecycle methods. `TechRequestController` is the thin HTTP facade with fourteen methods covering per-WO list/create, cross-cutting list with filters, single-request CRUD + four lifecycle transitions (approve/decline/cancel/fulfil), per-WO additional-tech list/add, and single-tech update/remove; `declineRequest` validates the rejection_reason in the request body and raises InvalidArgumentException when blank or absent so the route layer's error middleware can emit a 400 instead of leaking a generic 500; `removeTech` extracts removal_reason from the body (optional — the service accepts null reason for the "no comment" case but stamps removed_at regardless). New module wired via `routes/modules/workorder_tech_requests.php` and registered in `routes/api.php`'s loader array (after `workorder_change_orders`). Fourteen routes total: `GET /api/workorders/{id}/tech-requests` (per-WO list — workorder id in path so URL-keyed authorization can be added later without payload changes), `POST /api/workorders/{id}/tech-requests` (file a new request against a specific WO), `GET /api/workorder-tech-requests` (cross-cutting list — filters by status/urgency/requestor for the dispatch board's "incoming queue" widget), `GET/PUT /api/workorder-tech-requests/{id}` (single-request fetch + edit, using the resource id rather than nested under WO so a deep link to a specific request doesn't need the WO id), four lifecycle endpoints `POST /api/workorder-tech-requests/{id}/approve`, `/decline`, `/cancel`, `/fulfil`, plus `GET/POST /api/workorders/{id}/additional-techs` (per-WO list with optional active_only filter + direct-add path), and `PUT /api/workorder-additional-techs/{id}` + `POST /api/workorder-additional-techs/{id}/remove` for single-tech metadata edits and soft-removal. Permission additions to `config/auth.php`: `workorder_tech_requests.view` + `.create` + `.manage` added to dispatcher (full owner — dispatch is where the workflow lives, dispatch reads the queue, files internal "we need an extra hand" requests on behalf of the field, AND makes the approve/decline/fulfil decisions); same three added to manager (managers cover dispatch when the dispatcher is unavailable); `view` + `create` added to technician (techs are the primary requestors but cannot approve their own requests — separation of duties from the dispatch-side decisions); `view` added to accounting (read-only for labor cost reconciliation). Test coverage: 37-case `TechRequestServiceTest` (SQLite-gated, consistent with prior phases) — model status const vocabulary + ALLOWED_TRANSITIONS map shape + REQUEST_TYPES + URGENCIES consts + ROLES - [x] WO reassignment request flow _(10.4 — migration 143 lays down two idempotent tables: `workorder_reassignment_requests` (workorder_id INT NOT NULL with FK CASCADE so deleting the parent WO sweeps its open reassignment requests automatically — guarded on the `workorders` table existing so the migration runs cleanly on a partially-set-up DB; requested_by_user_id INT NOT NULL with FK RESTRICT on `users` because the requestor IS the audit trail — silently nulling them would erase "who said they couldn't finish this WO" which is the entire point of the workflow, RESTRICT forces operator resolution before a user delete; current_assignee_user_id nullable + FK SET NULL — this is a SNAPSHOT of who was on the WO at request creation time, recorded so the audit can later show "this request was filed against tech 8 even though we ultimately reassigned to tech 9" — nullable because a WO can be filed for reassignment even when nobody is currently assigned, e.g., during a routing rebalance; proposed_assignee_user_id nullable + FK SET NULL — the requestor's optional suggestion of who should take over (e.g., "I think Dave handles HVAC better than me on this one") — separate from new_assignee_user_id which is what dispatch actually picks at fulfilment, since the suggestion may be ignored; reassignment_reason with a six-value vocabulary (`emergency_unavailable`|`skill_mismatch`|`scheduling_conflict`|`safety_concern`|`customer_request`|`other`) so dispatch reporting can distinguish "tech had a personal emergency" from "tech was the wrong specialist" from "tech was double-booked" from "tech can't safely complete due to equipment/site issue" from "customer requested someone else" — those five reasons drive different staffing decisions and a free-text-only reason would have made aggregation impossible; reason TEXT NOT NULL because every reassignment request must carry a "why" beyond the category — anonymous "please reassign me" is not actionable; urgency with a four-value vocabulary (`low`|`normal`|`high`|`urgent`) so the dispatch board's "incoming reassignment requests" widget can sort the urgent ones to the top — explicitly NOT a free-form priority field because dispatchers need a fixed ladder; status with the five-state lifecycle (`pending`|`approved`|`declined`|`cancelled`|`fulfilled`) covering file → triage → outcome — the approved/fulfilled split is intentional because dispatch reality is "yes we can reassign this" often happens before "and here's the replacement" — the approval is the policy decision and fulfilment is the staffing decision; rejection_reason TEXT nullable but service-required when transitioning to declined so a refusal always carries documentation rather than vanishing; approved_by/declined_by/cancelled_by/fulfilled_by_user_id all nullable + FK SET NULL guarded on `users` so deleting a staff member doesn't blow away the audit of who-decided-what; lifecycle timestamps requested_at + approved_at + declined_at + cancelled_at + fulfilled_at all individually nullable so the audit can show "approved at 11:00, fulfilled at 13:00" without inventing fake timestamps for states the request never reached; new_assignee_user_id nullable + FK SET NULL — the actual replacement chosen at fulfilment time, distinct from proposed_assignee_user_id (suggestion) so the audit shows "tech proposed Dave but dispatch picked Eve"; notes TEXT for free-form internal commentary; timestamps; three indexes — workorder_id for the per-WO list page (techs and dispatchers both want "show me reassignment activity for this job"), status for the cross-WO "all pending reassignment requests awaiting dispatch decision" widget, and requested_by_user_id for "show me all my pending requests" on the tech's own dashboard. `workorder_reassignment_history` (workorder_id INT NOT NULL CASCADE guarded on `workorders` existing; request_id nullable + FK SET NULL on `workorder_reassignment_requests` because the direct dispatch path bypasses the request workflow — the history is the canonical "who has been the primary tech on this WO over time" view regardless of which path produced the row, AND because requests can be archived/deleted but the history must survive; from_user_id nullable + FK SET NULL on `users` — nullable because the very first reassignment of a WO might be FROM nobody (the WO was unassigned and dispatch picked an owner), and SET NULL because deleting the prior tech shouldn't destroy the audit of "Carol used to own this WO before she left"; to_user_id INT NOT NULL with FK RESTRICT on `users` because deleting the current owner mid-job needs to fail loudly so dispatch can reassign first rather than silently orphaning the WO's labor history; reassigned_by_user_id nullable + FK SET NULL — who pushed the button, distinct from from/to which are the affected parties; reassigned_at DATETIME NOT NULL — the actual moment the workorder row was mutated, used to chronologically reconstruct the WO's ownership timeline; reason VARCHAR(255) nullable — for the request-driven path the service writes a structured "{category}: {detail}" prefix here so a single-line audit view can render without joining back to the request, for the direct path it's whatever free-text reason the manager entered; notes TEXT for additional commentary; timestamps; three indexes — composite (workorder_id, reassigned_at) for the chronological per-WO history view (the hot read pattern is "show me the timeline for this WO"), from_user_id for "show me every WO that was reassigned away from user X" on the departing tech's handoff history, and to_user_id for "show me every WO that landed on user X via reassignment" on the receiving tech's onboarding history. The split between requests and history is load-bearing: the request is the workflow/approval object that lives for the duration of the triage decision (and may be cancelled/declined and never produce a history row), the history is the system-of-record for the actual ownership changes that hit the workorders row. This split lets us cleanly represent "emergency reassignment with no formal request" (history row, no request) AND "request that was declined and never fulfilled" (request row, no history) AND "request that was approved and fulfilled" (request row + history row linked via request_id). Two typed `BaseModel` subclasses — `WorkorderReassignmentRequest` carrying STATUSES const for vocabulary validation, REASONS + URGENCIES consts so those vocabularies are single-sourced from the model rather than scattered through validation paths, and an `ALLOWED_TRANSITIONS` const map (`approved` ← pending only; `declined` ← pending only; `fulfilled` ← approved only; `cancelled` ← pending OR approved) — declarative state machine so illegal jumps (declined → fulfilled, fulfilled → approved, cancelled → fulfilled) get rejected at validation rather than producing nonsensical lifecycle audit trails; the "fulfilled ← approved only" rule is the load-bearing one because it forces the policy decision (approval) to precede the staffing decision (fulfilment) and prevents the shortcut of dispatch silently flipping the WO ownership without an explicit yes/no on whether the request was warranted; `WorkorderReassignmentHistory` is a thin model with just the columns + autohydration since the history is append-only and has no behavioral surface beyond "make this row inspectable as a typed object". `ReassignmentRepository` follows the COLUMNS-const + private-hydrate pattern with two logical surfaces (requests + history) sharing one PDO connection; `loadCurrentAssignee(workorderId)` reads `workorders.assigned_technician_id` directly so the service can snapshot the current owner at request creation time AND re-read the live owner at fulfilment time (to detect drift if the WO was reassigned through some other path while the request was pending); `applyAssignment(workorderId, fromUserId, toUserId, reassignedBy, reassignedAt, requestId, reason, notes)` is the load-bearing transactional bridge — wraps `UPDATE workorders SET assigned_technician_id = :to, updated_at = CURRENT_TIMESTAMP` + `INSERT INTO workorder_reassignment_history` in a `beginTransaction/commit/rollBack` block so the two writes are atomic — a partial failure can never leave the WO row updated without a history record or the history record without the WO update; this is the single chokepoint through which BOTH the request-fulfilment path and the direct manager-reassignment path mutate the WO ownership, so a future audit query against the history table is guaranteed to be a complete record of every reassignment that ever happened; the transactional applyAssignment matters for production MySQL where concurrent writes to the WO row are real; SQLite tests don't exercise the rollback path but production behavior is preserved. `castRequestColumn` is a narrow casting helper that collapses empty-string inputs to NULL on nullable user-id columns + cast cents/ids to int + status fields to string so blank form submissions don't leave artifact whitespace and don't trip type-juggling bugs at the SQL boundary. `ReassignmentService` orchestrates the gates + validation + state machine + WO bridge: three permission gates (`workorder_reassignment_requests.view` for read paths, `workorder_reassignment_requests.create` for filing a new request, and `workorder_reassignment_requests.manage` for the dispatch decisions — approve/decline/fulfil + direct manager-reassignment — separation of duties matching the tech-request pattern from 10.3, so a tech with view+create can ASK to be reassigned but cannot stamp their own request as approved or unilaterally swap themselves off the job; managers + admin via `*` get all three; dispatcher gets all three because dispatch is the operational owner of the workflow; technician gets view + create because techs are the primary requestors but cannot make dispatch decisions on their own requests; accounting gets view-only for labor-attribution reporting since reassignments split billable hours across multiple primary techs over the WO's lifetime). `createRequest` validates required reason text + reassignment_reason vocabulary + urgency vocabulary, snapshots the current_assignee_user_id from `workorders.assigned_technician_id` at creation time so the audit captures who was being asked to be replaced, validates that proposed_assignee_user_id (when provided) is different from the current assignee (proposing yourself as the replacement is a no-op and meaningless), forces status='pending' and requested_at=now on creation regardless of payload (the only way to skip pending is via a lifecycle transition that records the corresponding timestamp). `updateRequest` is the metadata mutation path that **explicitly strips** status + all lifecycle timestamps + approved_by/declined_by/cancelled_by/fulfilled_by + new_assignee_user_id + rejection_reason from the input payload — these fields can only be moved through the dedicated lifecycle methods so the service stays the single writer of those columns; updateRequest also rejects edits when status is not pending (so an approved or declined request becomes immutable, preventing post-hoc rewriting of what was originally asked for and how dispatch responded), and re-validates the proposed-vs-current rule on edit. `approveRequest` requires `.manage` gate, validates pending → approved transition via assertTransitionAllowed, stamps approved_at + approved_by_user_id; `declineRequest` requires `.manage` gate, requires non-blank rejection_reason (a refusal with no reason is operationally useless — the requestor needs to know why), pending → declined, stamps declined_at + declined_by_user_id + rejection_reason; `cancelRequest` is the owner-friendly path — checks `requested_by_user_id === actor->id` and if true allows cancel WITHOUT requiring the manage gate (cancelling your OWN request is a routine operation, not a dispatch decision, e.g., the tech's emergency cleared up before dispatch could act), only when the actor is NOT the original requestor does cancelRequest fall through to the gate.assert(.manage) check so a dispatcher cancelling someone else's request still goes through authorization; pending|approved → cancelled, stamps cancelled_at + cancelled_by_user_id (so audit can show "cancelled by self" vs "cancelled by dispatcher"); `fulfilRequest` requires `.manage` gate and `new_assignee_user_id` in payload (dispatch must explicitly name the replacement — no implicit "the next available tech" magic), validates approved → fulfilled transition (cannot fulfil a pending request — must approve first, deliberate two-step), re-reads the WO's current assignee at fulfilment time via loadCurrentAssignee (this matters for the drift case where the WO was reassigned through some OTHER path while the request was pending — fulfilment must use the LIVE current as from_user_id, not the stale snapshot stored on the request), explicitly rejects fulfilling with the same user as the live current (no-op reassignment must not produce a history row), then within applyAssignment's atomic transaction stamps the request fulfilled (status + fulfilled_at + fulfilled_by_user_id + new_assignee_user_id) AND mutates the WO row AND inserts the history record — this auto-creation is what makes fulfilment a single atomic dispatch action rather than two separate API calls. `reassignDirectly` is the manager-bypass path for "a tech is in a car accident and dispatch needs to redistribute their queue NOW" — same applyAssignment chokepoint under the hood, requires .manage gate, requires new_assignee_user_id, rejects no-op (current === new), records request_id=null in the history so the audit can distinguish request-driven vs direct reassignments. The `?DateTimeImmutable $now` params on all lifecycle methods are for tests — production calls leave them null and let the service generate `new DateTimeImmutable()` at transition time; tests pass deterministic timestamps so the assertions can check exact stamp values. `assertTransitionAllowed(from, to)` consults ALLOWED_TRANSITIONS[to] and rejects if `from` is not in the allowed-from list — single declarative source of truth for what moves are legal so adding a new state in the future is one map edit rather than scattered if/else throughout the lifecycle methods. `shortReasonLabel(req)` is the helper that constructs the structured "{category}: {detail}" prefix the request-driven fulfilment writes into the history's reason column, capped at 240 chars to fit the VARCHAR(255) so a single-line audit view can render the categorized reason without joining back to the request. `ReassignmentController` is the thin HTTP facade with twelve methods covering per-WO request list/create + per-WO history list + direct reassignment, cross-cutting request list with filters, single-request CRUD + four lifecycle transitions (approve/decline/cancel/fulfil); `declineRequest` validates the rejection_reason in the request body and raises InvalidArgumentException when blank or absent so the route layer's error middleware can emit a 400 instead of leaking a generic 500; `reassignDirectly` returns the new history row in the data envelope so the UI can immediately render the audit entry without a follow-up GET. New module wired via `routes/modules/workorder_reassignments.php` and registered in `routes/api.php`'s loader array (after `workorder_tech_requests`). Twelve routes total: `GET /api/workorders/{id}/reassignment-requests` (per-WO list), `POST /api/workorders/{id}/reassignment-requests` (file a new request against a specific WO), `GET /api/workorders/{id}/reassignment-history` (per-WO chronological audit log), `POST /api/workorders/{id}/reassign-now` (manager-direct path skipping the request workflow), `GET /api/workorder-reassignment-requests` (cross-cutting list — filters by status/urgency/requestor/reason for the dispatch board's "incoming queue" widget), `GET/PUT /api/workorder-reassignment-requests/{id}` (single-request fetch + edit, using the resource id rather than nested under WO so a deep link to a specific request doesn't need the WO id), four lifecycle endpoints `POST /api/workorder-reassignment-requests/{id}/approve`, `/decline`, `/cancel`, `/fulfil`. Permission additions to `config/auth.php`: `workorder_reassignment_requests.view` + `.create` + `.manage` added to dispatcher (full owner — dispatch is where the workflow lives, dispatch reads the queue, files reassignment requests on behalf of techs in the field, AND makes the approve/decline/fulfil decisions); same three added to manager (managers cover dispatch when the dispatcher is unavailable + handle the direct emergency-reassignment path themselves); `view` + `create` added to technician (techs are the primary requestors but cannot approve their own requests — separation of duties from the dispatch-side decisions); `view` added to accounting (read-only for labor attribution reporting since reassignments split billable hours across multiple primary techs over the WO's lifetime). Test coverage: 38-case `ReassignmentServiceTest` (SQLite-gated, consistent with prior phases) — model status const vocabulary + ALLOWED_TRANSITIONS map shape + REASONS const, createRequest validates required reason and rejects empty, createRequest rejects unknown reassignment_reason category, createRequest rejects unknown urgency, createRequest snapshots current_assignee_user_id from workorders.assigned_technician_id (verified by inspecting the persisted column after create), createRequest handles unassigned WO (current snapshot stays null when no prior tech), createRequest rejects proposed_assignee_user_id === current (no-op suggestion), createRequest accepts proposed when different from current, createRequest stamps requested_at, approveRequest moves pending → approved + stamps approved_at + approved_by, approveRequest rejects already-approved request (illegal pending-only transition), declineRequest requires non-blank reason (whitespace → reject), declineRequest moves pending → declined with reason + declined_at + declined_by stamped, declineRequest blocked after approval (legal-from rule), cancelRequest by OWNER works without manage permission (verified with actor=requestor + .manage denied), cancelRequest by NON-OWNER requires manage (verified with actor!=requestor + .manage denied → UnauthorizedException), cancelRequest from approved still works (cancelling an approved-but-not-yet-fulfilled request is valid when the situation changed), cancelRequest rejected from fulfilled state (already swapped, must reverse via a new reassignment), fulfilRequest requires new_assignee_user_id in payload, fulfilRequest rejects fulfilling a pending request (must approve first — two-step deliberate gate), fulfilRequest atomically updates workorders.assigned_technician_id (verified by querying the WO row after fulfilment to confirm the swap), fulfilRequest stamps fulfilled_at + fulfilled_by + new_assignee_user_id on the request, fulfilRequest appends a history row with from=prior, to=new, reassigned_by=dispatcher, request_id linkage to the originating request, and reason carrying the structured "{category}: {detail}" prefix so single-line audit views render without join-backs, fulfilRequest rejects when new_assignee_user_id is the current assignee (no-op fulfilment), **drift handling verified** — when WO was directly reassigned (8→10) AFTER the request was filed (snapshot=8) but BEFORE fulfilment, fulfilment uses the LIVE current (10) as from_user_id rather than the stale snapshot (8), so the history chain is chronologically coherent (8→10 via direct, then 10→9 via fulfilment), reassignDirectly requires new_assignee_user_id, reassignDirectly rejects no-op (current === new), reassignDirectly updates workorders + appends history with request_id=null + records the manager-supplied reason, reassignDirectly handles unassigned WO (from_user_id stays null when WO had no prior tech), updateRequest strips status + all lifecycle fields from payload (verified by trying to set status=fulfilled + approved_at + new_assignee_user_id through update — all stripped, only reason + urgency survive), updateRequest blocked after approval (already-approved request rejects update), updateRequest rejects setting proposed_assignee_user_id === current_assignee (re-validates the no-op rule on edit), createRequest permission denied without .create gate, listRequestsForWorkorder permission denied without .view gate, approveRequest permission denied without .manage gate, fulfilRequest permission denied without .manage gate, reassignDirectly permission denied without .manage gate, list filters by status (pending vs approved both surface only matching rows), list filters by urgency (urgent + normal both surface only matching), list filters by reassignment_reason category (safety_concern + skill_mismatch both surface only matching), history lists in chronological order with from-user chains correctly across multiple hops (8→9, then 9→10 — second hop's from is the previous hop's to), controller create returns data envelope including the snapshot of current_assignee_user_id, controller decline requires rejection_reason in body (raises InvalidArgumentException when omitted), controller history returns data envelope, controller reassign-now returns the freshly inserted history row with the structured reason. All nine new/modified files lint green via `php -l`; autoload regenerated successfully (939 → 944 classes — five new: WorkorderReassignmentRequest, WorkorderReassignmentHistory, ReassignmentRepository, ReassignmentService, ReassignmentController; all loadable via the project autoloader).)_
- [x] AI triage suggestions (ticket sentiment/urgency) _(10.5 — migration 144 lays down one idempotent table: `ticket_triage_suggestions` (ticket_id INT NOT NULL with FK CASCADE so deleting a ticket sweeps its full suggestion history automatically — guarded on the `tickets` table existing so the migration runs cleanly on a partially-set-up DB; generated_at DATETIME NOT NULL stamping when the scorer ran — distinct from created_at which records when the row was persisted, so a future "scored at 14:32 but persisted at 14:33 due to write queue lag" scenario can be reconstructed; generated_by VARCHAR(80) NOT NULL DEFAULT 'heuristic_v1' identifying which scorer produced the suggestion — the value is the scorer's `label()` and is the join key for downstream model-evaluation reports that want to slice accuracy by provider/version (e.g., "what fraction of openai_gpt4_v3 suggestions were accepted vs rejected over the last 30 days"); suggested_priority VARCHAR(40) nullable carrying values from the same vocabulary as `Ticket::priority` (`p1_critical`|`p2_high`|`p3_normal`|`p4_low`) — nullable because not every scorer outputs a priority recommendation (a sentiment-only scorer might leave it null); suggested_category_id INT nullable + FK SET NULL on `ticket_categories` because the suggestion is purely a recommendation — deleting a category shouldn't blow away the suggestion record itself, just orphan the pointer so downstream evaluation reports can still ask "of suggestions whose category was later deleted, what fraction were accepted while the category still existed" without losing the row entirely; suggested_assignee_user_id INT nullable + FK SET NULL on `users` for the same reason — deleting a tech shouldn't lose the audit of "the model wanted to route this to Carol back when Carol was active"; sentiment_score DECIMAL(4,3) nullable on the −1.000 to +1.000 axis (negative=hostile, positive=positive) — DECIMAL not FLOAT because the comparison "sentiment <= -0.40" used by the heuristic priority bumper must be exact across MySQL/SQLite without float-drift surprises; urgency_score DECIMAL(4,3) on the 0.000 to 1.000 axis with the same precision guarantee; confidence DECIMAL(4,3) on the 0.000 to 1.000 axis representing how sure the scorer is about its overall recommendation — used by the triage queue UI to render a "review carefully" badge for low-confidence suggestions and to feed model-quality dashboards that bucket "confident-and-accepted" vs "confident-and-rejected" vs "low-confidence-and-accepted" so a tuning team can see whether confidence is well-calibrated; reasoning TEXT nullable carrying the human-readable trace of which rules fired (heuristic) or the LLM's reasoning content (AI provider) — useful for triagers debugging why the scorer pinned an apparently benign-looking ticket as p1, AND useful for prompt-engineering iteration when an AI scorer's recommendations are systematically off; status with the four-state lifecycle (`pending`|`accepted`|`rejected`|`stale`) covering generated → triage decision → terminal — the stale state is novel here and load-bearing: re-running the scorer on the same ticket marks any prior pending suggestion as 'stale' rather than deleting it, so the history is preserved for model-evaluation reports while the triage queue widget only ever surfaces the LATEST pending suggestion per ticket — without 'stale', either the queue would show duplicate stale rows OR the history would be lost on every re-score; accepted_by_user_id + accepted_at + applied_changes (JSON-encoded snapshot of what fields actually got written back to the ticket — capturing the audit because the triager might selectively accept only the priority recommendation and leave the suggested category/assignee unapplied, and applied_changes records the actual writes so a future audit can show "the model suggested {priority, category, assignee} but only priority was applied"); rejected_by_user_id + rejected_at + rejection_reason for the negative-feedback path; notes TEXT for free-form internal commentary; timestamps; three indexes — ticket_id for the per-ticket suggestion history view (the sidebar widget on the ticket detail page that shows "previous triage suggestions for this ticket" sorted newest first), status for the cross-cutting "all pending suggestions awaiting review" triage queue widget, and generated_at for the model-evaluation reports that bucket suggestions by date range). Three new files in `src/Services/Tickets/` form the scorer abstraction: `TriageScore.php` is a readonly DTO carrying suggestedPriority + suggestedCategoryId + suggestedAssigneeUserId + sentimentScore + urgencyScore + confidence + reasoning — kept separate from the persisted `TicketTriageSuggestion` model so scorer implementations don't need to know anything about persistence mechanics, they just inspect a Ticket and return a verdict; all fields are nullable because not every scorer outputs every signal (a heuristic might compute urgency from keywords but have nothing meaningful to say about sentiment, while an LLM-backed scorer might return all three plus a free-text reasoning trace). `TicketTriageScorerInterface` defines two methods — `score(Ticket): TriageScore` and `label(): string` — the two-method shape lets the service write the scorer's stable identifier into the persisted suggestion's `generated_by` column without needing to know what kind of scorer is plugged in (`'heuristic_v1'` for the shipped impl, `'openai_gpt4_v3'` or `'anthropic_claude_v1'` for AI providers swapped in at the DI binding), and the join key gives downstream model-evaluation reports a stable group-by axis. `HeuristicTicketTriageScorer` is the shipped default — rule-based keyword + polarity analysis, deterministic, no external calls, always available even when no AI provider is configured. The scorer's URGENCY_TOKENS array is intentionally tilted toward auto-repair / service-ticket vocabulary ("no brakes", "smoking", "leak", "stranded", "unsafe", "collision") because in a generic help-desk corpus those words wouldn't carry the same weight, but in the domain this codebase ships into they're load-bearing signals — a "no brakes" ticket is operationally a P1 even if the customer phrases it calmly. URGENCY_HIT (0.30) and DEADLINE_HIT (0.15) are the per-token weights — a single major urgency keyword pushes the score to 0.30 (p3 bucket), three urgency keywords clamp to 0.85+ (p1), and deadline phrases ("asap", "by tomorrow") add smaller bumps because deadline pressure is a softer signal than safety-keyword urgency. The `priorityFromUrgency(urgency, sentiment)` rule maps urgency to priority via three thresholds (>=0.85 → p1, >=0.60 → p2, >=0.30 → p3, else p4) WITH a sentiment-bumper rule that lifts a p3 to p2 when sentiment is strongly negative (<= -0.40) — the operational logic being that an angry but non-urgent customer is still worth expedited review, because a frustrated customer who feels ignored at p3 today is a churned customer next month. `confidenceFromHits` ramps from a 0.20 floor up to 1.00 based on how many strong signals fired (each urgency hit adds 0.20 with a 0.60 cap so 3+ hits saturate the urgency contribution, plus a 0.15 bump if sentiment fired at all) — keeping the confidence model simple is intentional because for a rule-based scorer "confidence" is really "did multiple rules agree" and that's easy to enumerate; an AI provider implementing the interface would derive confidence from log-probabilities or self-reported certainty. `reasoning` is the human-readable trace joined with " | " — gives triagers exactly enough information to second-guess the model: "urgency keywords: fire, smoke | sentiment tokens: -frustrated | priority bucket: p1_critical" tells the triager which inputs drove the recommendation so they can decide whether the keyword match was spurious (e.g., "fire" matched on "fire alarm test" which is benign). Repository `TicketTriageRepository` follows the COLUMNS-const + private-hydrate pattern; key non-CRUD operations are `markPriorPendingStale(ticketId)` (bulk UPDATE `... SET status='stale' WHERE ticket_id=:t AND status='pending'`, returns rowCount so the service can include it in audit logs — called BEFORE inserting a freshly-scored suggestion so the queue widget never shows two pending rows for the same ticket) and `listPending(filters)` (cross-cutting feed for the triage queue widget, newest-first via id desc, optionally filtered by `generated_by` so a dispatch environment running multiple scorers can show one queue per scorer). The `castColumn` casting helper handles the DECIMAL columns specially via `nullableFloat` so empty-string inputs from form posts become NULL rather than 0.0 — important because 0.0 sentiment is "neutral" while NULL is "scorer didn't produce a sentiment value", and conflating them would corrupt downstream model-evaluation aggregations. `TicketTriageService` orchestrates the gates + state machine + ticket-write side-effects via three permission gates (`ticket_triage.view` for read paths, `ticket_triage.generate` for running the scorer + persisting a suggestion, and `ticket_triage.manage` for accept/reject decisions — separation of duties matching the prior phases so a viewer can read suggestions but can't run new ones, and a generator can create suggestions without authority to accept/reject; managers + admin via `*` get all three; dispatcher gets all three because dispatch IS the triage queue owner; technician gets view-only because techs need to see why a ticket they're working on was pinned as p1 but shouldn't be running scorers or accepting/rejecting; accounting gets view-only for model-quality audits that compare scorer recommendations against actual outcomes). `generateForTicket(actor, ticketId)` is the load-bearing path — loads the ticket via TicketRepository, fails fast if the ticket doesn't exist (rather than silently scoring against a phantom ticket and producing an orphan suggestion), calls `markPriorPendingStale(ticketId)` BEFORE the new INSERT so the staleness sweep happens in a defined order (any concurrent generation race ends with both new rows pending and only one will be the latest by id, which is the right tiebreaker for the queue widget), invokes `scorer.score(ticket)` to produce a TriageScore, then validates the suggested priority against `TicketTriageSuggestion::SUGGESTED_PRIORITIES` and clamps to NULL if the scorer returned an out-of-vocabulary value (defensive against a buggy AI provider inventing "p99_critical" — clamping to null rather than throwing means the scorer's other signals like sentiment/urgency/reasoning still get persisted for evaluation, but the bad priority doesn't poison the persistence layer). Persists the row with status='pending', `generated_by` from the scorer's `label()`, and the validated TriageScore fields. `acceptSuggestion(actor, id, options)` is the load-bearing decision path — validates pending → accepted via assertTransitionAllowed, then composes the writeback to the ticket from the suggestion's recommended fields filtered by per-field `apply_priority`/`apply_category`/`apply_assignee` boolean options (each defaults true so "just accept" works without specifying flags). The applied_changes column is set to the JSON-encoded snapshot of WHAT was actually written — not WHAT WAS SUGGESTED — so an audit query against accepted suggestions can ask "of all accepted suggestions, what fraction had ALL recommended fields applied vs only some" without re-deriving from the suggestion+ticket cross-product. The writeback to tickets goes through `TicketRepository::update($ticketId, $update)` which already handles the writable-fields whitelist and the Ticket model hydration on the return path, so the service doesn't need its own SQL for the ticket mutation — clean composition with the existing repo means future ticket-update validation (e.g., a future "priority change requires audit log entry" middleware) automatically applies to accept-driven writes too. The accepted_by_user_id + accepted_at fields are stamped from the actor and the optional `?DateTimeImmutable $now` param; the optional `notes` payload field overrides any pre-existing notes on the suggestion (so a triager can leave a contextual comment when accepting like "applied because customer called and confirmed" without overwriting the scorer's reasoning trace). `rejectSuggestion(actor, id, reason)` validates pending → rejected, requires non-blank rejection_reason (a refusal with no reason is operationally useless — it's the negative training signal that rejection produces, and a blank reason carries no signal), stamps rejected_by_user_id + rejected_at + rejection_reason. The `?DateTimeImmutable $now` params on generate/accept/reject are for tests — production calls leave them null and let the service generate `new DateTimeImmutable()` at action time; tests pass deterministic timestamps so the assertions can check exact stamp values. `TicketTriageController` is the thin HTTP facade with six methods covering per-ticket history list, generation trigger, cross-cutting pending queue, single-suggestion fetch, and accept/reject decisions; `acceptSuggestion` selectively pulls the three apply_* boolean flags out of the request body (only forwarding them as options when explicitly present — letting absent flags fall through to the service's default-true behavior) so a UI sending `{"apply_priority": false}` correctly suppresses the priority writeback while still applying category/assignee defaults; `rejectSuggestion` validates the rejection_reason in the body and raises InvalidArgumentException when blank or absent so the route layer's error middleware can emit a 400 instead of leaking a generic 500. New module wired via `routes/modules/ticket_triage.php` and registered in `routes/api.php`'s loader array (after `workorder_reassignments`). The route module DI-binds `HeuristicTicketTriageScorer` as the default — to swap in an AI provider in production, replace the line `$scorer = new HeuristicTicketTriageScorer();` with whatever implementation of TicketTriageScorerInterface the install ships, and the rest of the route+service pipeline picks it up unchanged. Six routes total: `GET /api/tickets/{id}/triage-suggestions` (per-ticket history list — ticket id in path so URL-keyed authorization can be added later without payload changes), `POST /api/tickets/{id}/triage-suggestions/generate` (run the scorer + persist + stale prior pending — POST not GET because generation is a state-mutating action even when the read of the ticket is idempotent), `GET /api/ticket-triage-suggestions` (cross-cutting pending queue widget — filters by `generated_by` so multi-scorer environments can split queues by provider), `GET /api/ticket-triage-suggestions/{id}` (fetch single — uses the resource id rather than nested under ticket so a deep link to a specific suggestion doesn't need the ticket id), `POST /api/ticket-triage-suggestions/{id}/accept`, `POST /api/ticket-triage-suggestions/{id}/reject`. Permission additions to `config/auth.php`: `ticket_triage.view` + `.generate` + `.manage` added to dispatcher (full owner — dispatch IS the triage queue, dispatch reads the queue, runs scorers, accepts/rejects suggestions, applies recommended priority/category/assignee back to tickets via the accept path); same three added to manager (managers cover dispatch when the dispatcher is unavailable + can clear backlog overnight or for off-shift queues); `view` added to technician (techs read the suggestion to understand why a ticket they're working got pinned as p1 — useful when reviewing whether to push back on the priority — but cannot run scorers or accept/reject); `view` added to accounting (read-only for model-quality audits that bucket "scorer recommended X, triager accepted X / applied X / overrode X" so the team can see how often the scorer's recommendations got applied vs overridden, feeding model-quality dashboards). Test coverage: 30+-case `TicketTriageServiceTest` (SQLite-gated, consistent with prior phases) — model status const vocabulary + ALLOWED_TRANSITIONS map shape (accepted/rejected/stale all from-pending only, no terminal-to-terminal jumps allowed) + SUGGESTED_PRIORITIES vocabulary, heuristic scorer label() returns 'heuristic_v1', heuristic produces p1_critical on safety+outage keywords (truck on fire + smoke + dangerous + asap → urgency >= 0.85 → p1), heuristic produces p1/p2 on no-brakes ticket (deterministic safety keyword), heuristic produces p4_low on benign questions (no urgency keywords, no sentiment tokens → falls through to p4), heuristic sentiment-bumper rule verified (1 urgency keyword + strong negative sentiment → p3 bumped to p2), heuristic sentiment_score is null when no tokens match (preserves "scorer didn't produce a sentiment" semantics rather than collapsing to 0.0), heuristic confidence grows with signal count (weak ticket has lower confidence than strong ticket), heuristic reasoning trace is non-empty and includes "urgency keywords" prefix when keywords matched (so triagers can see what fired), repo listForTicket returns descending by id (newest first matches queue UX), repo markPriorPendingStale only targets pending rows (does NOT stale already-accepted rows, verified by a 3-row fixture with mixed statuses), repo listPending filters by generated_by (multi-scorer fixture verifies the heuristic-only filter excludes the openai_v2 row), generateForTicket persists the suggestion with the scorer's label baked into generated_by + correct ticket_id + status='pending' + correct generated_at + suggested_priority='p1_critical' for a fire ticket, generateForTicket rejects unknown ticket id (fails fast rather than producing an orphan suggestion), generateForTicket marks prior pending stale on re-score (verified by a two-pass fixture: first generate is staled, second is the live pending), generateForTicket clamps unknown priority to null (canned scorer returning 'p99_critical' produces persisted row with suggested_priority=null — defensive against buggy AI providers), generateForTicket persists the scorer's custom label (canned 'openai_gpt4_v3' label appears in generated_by), acceptSuggestion writes priority back to the ticket (verified by re-reading the ticket via repo), acceptSuggestion writes category + assignee back when scorer recommended them (canned scorer with categoryId=42 + assigneeId=99 produces ticket with those values populated), acceptSuggestion supports selective apply (apply_priority=true + recommended fields is no-op on the ticket (canned scorer returning empty TriageScore → ticket unchanged, applied_changes JSON is {} empty object), acceptSuggestion blocked when already accepted recommendations), rejectSuggestion records reason + stamps rejected_by/rejected_at, rejectSuggestion requires non-blank reason (whitespace → reject), rejectSuggestion blocked when already rejected (illegal transition guard), accept blocked after reject (proves the from-pending-only rule is symmetric — a rejected suggestion cannot be re-accepted), all four permission gates verified (view denied → listForTicket throws Unauthorized, generate denied → generateForTicket throws, manage denied → both accept and reject throw separately so the gates aren't conflated), service-level listForTicket when omitted), controller listPending returns the data envelope with the array of toArray'd suggestions. - [x] Route optimization + geo-fencing _(10.6 — migration 145 lays down four idempotent tables in one ship: `geo_fences` (spatial regions used for membership tests against position updates from mobile clients — the foundation for auto-clock-in workflows, "tech left site mid-job" alerts, after-the-fact "where did Carol's truck go today" reports), `geo_fence_events` (append-only audit log of (user, fence, entered/exited/dwell, occurred_at) tuples — never updated or deleted in the normal flow because the table IS the audit trail; the only mutation paths are explicit inserts from the mobile client and the auto-evaluator, and the only reads are dispatch's "show me the trail" view + downstream consumers like auto-clock-in that listen on the event stream), `route_plans` (a planned day of stops for one technician on one date with full optimization metadata — method/distance/duration/optimized_at — so a re-optimization can be triggered without losing the prior plan's audit trail; lifecycle draft → active → completed with a cancelled escape hatch from either non-terminal state), `route_plan_stops` (ordered stops within a route plan, each carrying planned-vs-actual arrival/departure timestamps for after-action analysis like "we estimated 3 hours but it took 4.5 — re-tune service_minutes_planned"). The migration uses the established information_schema-guarded PREPARE/EXECUTE pattern for idempotency on indexes + foreign keys; FKs are guarded on the referenced-table existence check (customers, workorders, users) so the migration stays safe on partially-set-up databases. Latitude/longitude columns use DECIMAL(10,7) — 7 decimal places gives ~1cm precision at the equator, plenty for routing — over FLOAT specifically because the haversine threshold checks (`distance <= radius_meters`) and the equality semantics of "is this point inside this fence" benefit from exact arithmetic rather than IEEE-754 drift across MySQL/SQLite. FK behavior: geo_fences.customer/workorder/asset are SET NULL because a fence outlives the tagged relationship for historical analysis (we want to keep "this fence used to belong to a customer that's now deleted" in the record rather than blow away the fence row). geo_fence_events.geo_fence_id is CASCADE because deleting a fence sweeps its event log (the events have no meaning without the fence), but geo_fence_events.user_id is RESTRICT because the user IS part of the audit trail and deleting them needs to fail loudly so dispatch can reassign the historical attribution. route_plans.planned_for_user_id is RESTRICT for the same reason — deleting a tech mid-day with active plans must fail loudly so dispatch can cancel/reassign — but created_by_user_id is SET NULL because the creator's identity is informational, not load-bearing, after the plan exists. route_plan_stops.route_plan_id is CASCADE (stops have no meaning without the plan); workorder_id is SET NULL (a deleted WO shouldn't blow away the stop's historical record of "we visited this place at this time"). Three indexes per table cover the dominant read paths: geo_fences gets purpose/customer/active (the picker for "show me all active service-zone fences" + the per-customer site lookup + the active-only filter that the evaluator hits on every position fix); geo_fence_events gets fence/user/occurred_at (the per-fence + per-user + time-range trail views); route_plans gets user + a composite (user, plan_date) covering "show me Carol's plan for today" — the dispatch-board hot read that fires per page — plus a status filter; route_plan_stops gets plan + a composite (plan, sequence_order) for the canonical ordered-render view + a status filter. Four BaseModel subclasses mirror the schema: GeoFence with SHAPES const (`circle`|`polygon`) and PURPOSES const (`shop_zone`|`customer_site`|`service_zone`|`restricted_zone`) — purpose drives downstream behavior so it's a first-class enum: shop_zone fences trigger clock-in candidates on entered events, customer_site fences on an active WO auto-stamp "arrived" on the relevant route stop, service_zone is informational coverage-reporting only (no events fire), restricted_zone fires alerts to dispatch on entered (off-limits area like a competitor lot). GeoFenceEvent with EVENT_TYPES (`entered`|`exited`|`dwell`) and SOURCES (`mobile_gps`|`manual`|`background_sync`) — sources distinguish how the position was reported because dispatch needs to know whether the event came from a live foreground/background GPS fix vs a dispatch backfill vs a queued offline-mode replay (operationally, "manual" events should be reviewed differently than auto-emitted ones). RoutePlan with STATUSES + ALLOWED_TRANSITIONS map (`draft`→`active`|`cancelled`, `active`→`completed`|`cancelled`, terminal states have empty transitions — once dispatched, "moving back to draft" is better modeled as cancel + create rather than rewinding a live plan) and OPTIMIZATION_METHODS const (`manual`|`nearest_neighbor`) which is the optimizer's `label()` and serves as the join key for downstream A/B reports comparing optimizer outcomes ("for plans optimized by nearest_neighbor vs or_tools, what was the average actual-vs-estimated duration variance"). RoutePlanStop with STATUSES (`planned`|`en_route`|`arrived`|`completed`|`skipped`) and ALLOWED_TRANSITIONS allowing forward path (planned→en_route→arrived→completed) plus skipped-from-any-non-terminal escape (no-show, can't access, dispatch reroute); rollback (en_route→planned) is intentionally NOT in the standard transition table — that path is dispatcher-only override handled at the service layer rather than as a normal transition, because once a tech started moving the audit trail should record the in-progress state rather than silently rewinding. The pure-logic GeoFenceEvaluator is stateless on purpose: every method takes its inputs as arguments so the heuristic is fully unit-testable without needing a database, AND so the mobile-side rule (where we may eventually want to evaluate fences offline for instant entered/exited UX) can use the same algorithm. `contains(fence, lat, lng)` returns false for inactive fences without checking geometry (callers can pass mixed lists without pre-filtering), checks circle membership via haversine great-circle distance compared against radius_meters, and checks polygon membership via ray-casting against the GeoJSON-ordered (`[lng, lat]`) ring with implicit closure (the algorithm doesn't require first==last). Earth radius constant is the WGS84 mean (6371008m) — sufficient for the few-hundred-meter scale we actually care about (a customer site fence is rarely larger than 200m). `matchingFences(fences, lat, lng)` is the bulk-filter helper the event service uses on every position fix. The pluggable RouteOptimizerInterface contract lets new optimizers (or-tools binding, OSRM-driven, manual-edited) plug in via the `routes/modules/routing.php` DI binding without touching the service layer. The shipped NearestNeighborRouteOptimizer is a greedy O(n²) heuristic — start at the origin, repeatedly pick the unvisited stop closest to the current position, move to it, repeat. Not optimal, but for the typical 8-12-stop technician day it's within ~10-15% of optimal and runs in microseconds. Travel time estimate is `distance / AVG_SPEED_KMH` (40 km/h default) plus the cumulative service minutes — real-world routing (OSRM, Google Directions) belongs downstream when actually dispatching; this is a planning-time approximation. RouteStop is a readonly DTO (id nullable so "preview optimize this proposal before saving" reads work the same as "re-optimize an existing plan" reads); RouteOptimizationResult carries the reordered stop list plus the totals.
