GeoFenceRepository follows the COLUMNS-const + private-hydrate pattern with `listActive` as the dominant read path (every position evaluation pulls the active list, so the indexed `active`+`purpose` columns make this cheap) plus `listAll(include_inactive)` for the dispatch admin view. GeoFenceEventRepository is append-only — only `create()` mutates; `findMostRecentForUserAndFence` is the dedup-check helper the auto-evaluator hits to avoid emitting a second 'entered' when the user is still inside the fence on the next position update. RoutePlanRepository combines plans + stops in one repo because they're tightly coupled (cascade delete + always-loaded-together) — separate COLUMNS consts, separate cast helpers, but unified hydration. `listForUserOnDate` is the dispatch-board hot read backed by the composite index; `listStopsForPlan` orders by sequence_order ASC for canonical render. GeoFenceService gates CRUD via `geofences.view` + `geofences.manage` and validates shape geometry up-front: a circle without center+radius is rejected (rather than letting the database accept a half-formed row that the evaluator can never match), a polygon without polygon_geojson is rejected, polygon rings under 3 points are rejected. Update-path validation merges into the existing row before validation, so a partial PATCH that sets only `radius_meters` is checked against the persisted `shape_type` rather than against the partial payload alone. GeoFenceEventService gates event reads + recording via `geofences.view` + `geofences.record_event`, with the load-bearing path being `recordPosition(actor, position)` — the auto-evaluator that takes a (user, lat, lng) fix, evaluates it against the active fence set, and emits an `entered` event for every fence the user is now inside that they weren't already inside on the most recent prior fix. The dedup uses `findMostRecentForUserAndFence` rather than tracking per-fix state externally — if the most recent event for (user, fence) was 'entered' or 'dwell', the user is still inside, suppress; otherwise emit. Exit events are not auto-emitted in 10.6 — that requires a per-fix "user was here last" cache that lives in a follow-up phase (the cleanest implementation needs to track the previous evaluation's match set, which adds either a redis dependency or a per-user state row). `recordExplicit(actor, data)` is the manual path used by the mobile "I'm here" button + dispatch backfill corrections; it validates event_type + source + fence existence + non-zero user_id before writing. `evaluatePosition(actor, lat, lng)` is the cheap pure helper that returns matching active fences without persisting an event — useful for the mobile UI rendering "you are inside customer-site-42" as a passive indicator. RoutePlanService gates reads + management + execution via three permission tiers (`route_plans.view` for read paths, `route_plans.manage` for create/update/delete plans + edit stops + optimize, `route_plans.execute` for field-side stamp arrived/departed/skipped on stops — separation of duties so a technician can complete their own stops without needing the dispatcher's full manage permission, but cannot reorder or delete the plan). Plan lifecycle methods (`activate`/`complete`/`cancel`) consult `RoutePlan::ALLOWED_TRANSITIONS` and stamp the corresponding lifecycle column (`activated_at`/`completed_at`/`cancelled_at`); the editable `updatePlan` strips `status` + lifecycle stamps from the writable payload so callers must go through the typed lifecycle methods to keep the timestamps in sync. `deletePlan` blocks deletion of an active plan (cancel-then-delete is the right path, since an active plan that vanishes silently leaves orphaned stop records that techs may still be looking at on their mobile clients). `addStop` auto-increments `sequence_order` if not specified (max+1) and refuses adds on completed/cancelled plans (would violate the "history is frozen" property of those terminal states). Stop-execution methods (`markStopEnRoute`/`markStopArrived`/`markStopCompleted`/`markStopSkipped`) consult `RoutePlanStop::ALLOWED_TRANSITIONS`, stamp `arrived_at` on arrived and `departed_at` on completed/skipped, accept an optional `?DateTimeImmutable $now` for test determinism, and call `maybeAutoCompletePlan` after the terminal transitions (completed/skipped) — a dedicated pass that re-reads the plan's stops and, if every stop has reached a terminal status (completed or skipped) and the plan is currently active, auto-stamps the plan as completed. This auto-complete is load-bearing UX: dispatch shouldn't have to manually mark every plan complete when the last stop hits done, and the rule "every stop terminal → plan terminal" is the natural definition. `optimize(planId)` is the marquee feature — re-optimizes the plan's stops in-place using the bound optimizer, but only touches stops still in `planned` status (you can't re-route around a tech who's already en_route to a stop, so anything past planned is "frozen" and keeps its existing sequence; the optimizer reorders only the planned stops and sequences them after the highest frozen sequence). Optimization writes the optimizer's `label()` into the plan's `optimization_method` column so the audit trail shows which algorithm produced the current order, plus stamps `total_distance_meters` + `total_duration_minutes` + `optimized_at`. The two-phase sequence rewrite (move all optimizable stops to high temporary slots first, then assign final order) is defensive: even though the SQLite test schema doesn't enforce a (plan, sequence) uniqueness, the production MySQL schema may add one in a future migration, and the two-phase pattern guarantees no transient sequence collision during the rewrite. The single RoutingController is a thin HTTP facade exposing the full surface for fences (5 routes), events (4 routes including the auto-position-evaluator and the pure evaluatePosition helper), plans (9 routes including activate/complete/cancel/optimize lifecycle moves), and stops (9 routes including the 4 execute-side stamps). One controller because dispatch's mental model is one feature ("plan the day, then track where techs actually went") and splitting into separate controllers would force the route file to wire two DI graphs that share most of their dependencies. All envelopes follow the `{"data": ...}` shape used by the rest of the API. The `getPlan` controller method returns the plan merged with its stops array (avoids a second round-trip from the dispatch board, which always shows the stops alongside the plan). `routes/modules/routing.php` wires the full DI graph: GeoFenceEvaluator (shared by both event service AND optimizer), three repositories, NearestNeighborRouteOptimizer as the default optimizer, three services, and the unified controller. To swap optimizers in production, replace the binding line in routes/modules/routing.php with whatever implementation of RouteOptimizerInterface the install ships — the rest of the route+service pipeline picks it up unchanged. 27 routes total: 5 fence CRUD + 4 event endpoints + 9 plan endpoints (CRUD + lifecycle + optimize + per-plan stops list/add) + 9 stop endpoints (CRUD + arrived/en-route/completed/skipped). Wired into routes/api.php via the loader array (after `ticket_triage`). Permission additions to `config/auth.php`: dispatcher gets `geofences.view` + `.manage` + `.record_event` plus `route_plans.view` + `.manage` + `.execute` (full owner — dispatch IS the routing surface, dispatch composes plans, dispatches them, manages fences, AND can stamp arrival/departure on a tech's behalf when the field side is offline); manager gets the same six permissions for off-shift coverage; technician gets `geofences.view` + `geofences.record_event` + `route_plans.view` + `route_plans.execute` (techs read the active fence list to render "you are inside customer-site" on mobile, post position fixes that auto-emit fence events, view their own assigned plans, and execute their own route stops via en_route → arrived → completed/skipped — fence + plan management stays with dispatch/manager); accounting gets `route_plans.view` + `geofences.view` (read-only for labor-attribution reports that correlate billed time against where the tech actually was per the route plan's actuals). Test coverage: 50+-case `RoutingServiceTest` (SQLite-gated, consistent with prior phases) — model status const vocabulary + ALLOWED_TRANSITIONS map shape (no terminal-to-terminal jumps allowed), evaluator haversine returns ~3,940km for NYC→LA (sanity check), evaluator haversine returns ~0 for same point, evaluator contains() returns true for circle center, returns true for ~110m east of center inside 500m radius, returns false for far north outside 100m radius, returns false for inactive fence regardless of geometry (proves the active-filter early-exit), returns false for circle missing geometry (defensive), returns true for polygon-square center, returns false for polygon-square far points (north + west), returns false for invalid polygon JSON (defensive), matchingFences returns only matching fences from a mixed list (1 of 2 in the fixture), repo create+findById round-trips a circle correctly (shape/purpose/radius preserved), repo listActive excludes inactive rows, repo listAll(include_inactive=true) returns both, service create rejects unknown shape_type, rejects circle missing radius, rejects polygon missing geojson, rejects polygon under 3 points (defensive geometry validation), service create requires geofences.manage, service list requires geofences.view, recordExplicit persists event with correct event_type+user+fence, rejects unknown event_type, rejects unknown fence id, recordPosition emits one entered event per matching active fence (verified by 2-fence overlap fixture), recordPosition dedups against the most recent prior entered (second fix at same location → 0 events emitted), recordPosition emits separately for different users (proves dedup is per-user), recordPosition returns empty when no fence matches, evaluatePosition returns active matches (pure read, no event written), optimizer label() returns 'nearest_neighbor', optimizer empty stops returns zero result short-circuit, optimizer picks nearest first then chains (verified by far-stop-first input that gets reordered to near-first), optimizer return_to_origin increases total distance, optimizer duration includes service minutes (30m service window + tiny travel → duration >= 30), createPlan defaults to draft status, createPlan requires planned_for_user_id, createPlan requires route_plans.manage, activate→complete lifecycle round-trip stamps activated_at + completed_at, cancel from draft works, cannot complete a draft plan (must activate first), cannot activate an already-completed plan (terminal-to-terminal blocked), cannot edit a completed plan, cannot delete an active plan (must cancel first), addStop auto-increments sequence_order, listStops returns rows in sequence_order ASC (verified by inserting B-then-A with sequences 2-then-1 — A comes back first), stop lifecycle planned→en_route→arrived→completed transitions and stamps arrived_at + departed_at, stop can skip from planned (skipped from any non-terminal state), cannot complete an already-completed stop (terminal-to-terminal blocked), stop execute requires route_plans.execute, plan auto-completes when the LAST stop reaches terminal (verified by 2-stop fixture: plan stays active until both stops are completed, then auto-stamps to completed), optimize rewrites sequence by proximity (far-then-near input becomes near-then-far output), optimize requires plan to have origin_latitude/origin_longitude set, optimize freezes non-planned stops (frozen arrived stop keeps its sequence; planned stops sequenced after the highest frozen sequence + ordered by proximity), optimize blocked on completed plan, controller getPlan envelope includes the stops array, controller listFences envelope shape verified. All 19 new files lint green via `php -l`; autoload regenerated successfully (951 → 967 classes — 16 new across Models/Routing service/controller/dtos: GeoFence, GeoFenceEvent, RoutePlan, RoutePlanStop, GeoFenceEvaluator, GeoFenceRepository, GeoFenceEventRepository, GeoFenceService, GeoFenceEventService, RoutePlanRepository, RoutePlanService, RouteOptimizerInterface, RouteOptimizationResult, NearestNeighborRouteOptimizer, RouteStop, RoutingController; all loadable via the project autoloader.)_
- [x] Voice-to-text notes _(10.7 — migration 146 lays down two idempotent tables in one ship: `voice_notes` (the recording metadata + transcript carrier — workorder_id INT NULL with FK CASCADE so deleting a WO sweeps its voice notes, ticket_id INT NULL with FK CASCADE so deleting a ticket sweeps its voice notes, vehicle_id INT NULL with FK SET NULL because voice notes can outlive the vehicle (notes about a totalled fleet unit are still useful retrospectively for failure analysis), author_user_id INT NULL with FK SET NULL so deleting an author preserves the note record with an orphaned author pointer for audit-trail integrity, audio_path VARCHAR(500) NOT NULL — the relative-to-storage-root path on the filesystem where the actual audio file lives, audio_format VARCHAR(20) NOT NULL DEFAULT 'mp3' constrained at the service layer to VoiceNote::SUPPORTED_FORMATS = ['mp3', 'wav', 'm4a', 'mp4', 'ogg', 'webm', 'aac', 'flac'] reflecting what the typical mobile client (React Native + AVAudioRecorder / android.media.MediaRecorder) emits by default, audio_size_bytes BIGINT NULL, duration_seconds DECIMAL(8,2) NULL populated either by the recorder (authoritative — it knows the actual file duration) or by the transcriber as a word-count-based estimate fallback when the recorder omits it, transcript TEXT NULL populated by the transcriber after transcription completes, transcript_language VARCHAR(20) NULL — BCP-47 tag (e.g., "en-US", "es-MX") detected by the transcriber or supplied as a hint by the recorder, transcription_provider VARCHAR(80) NOT NULL DEFAULT 'heuristic_v1' — stable identifier of the transcriber that produced the transcript ("heuristic_v1", "whisper_v1", "deepgram_v3") giving downstream model-quality reports a join key for slicing accuracy by provider/version, transcription_status VARCHAR(20) NOT NULL DEFAULT 'pending' driving a four-state state machine (pending → transcribing → completed/failed; failed → transcribing for retry; the model's STATUSES const publishes the vocabulary and ALLOWED_TRANSITIONS publishes the transition table), transcription_started_at and transcription_completed_at DATETIMEs stamped automatically by the service when the lifecycle moves so the row is self-describing without any external timing telemetry, transcription_failure_reason TEXT NULL captures the throwable message when a transcriber raises (operator can see why and either fix the audio or swap the transcriber binding), confidence DECIMAL(4,3) NULL — overall transcript confidence on a 0.0-1.0 scale (heuristic returns 1.0 for sidecar matches and 0.0 for placeholders; AI providers return their model's actual confidence), visibility VARCHAR(40) NOT NULL DEFAULT 'internal' constrained to ['internal', 'customer_visible'] — internal notes stay in dispatch UI, customer_visible notes flow to the customer portal alongside the WO, pinned TINYINT(1) NOT NULL DEFAULT 0 because operational notes ("this customer is hostile, two adults present") need to float to the top of the WO timeline regardless of how stale they are, notes TEXT NULL for the recorder's manual annotation overlay (e.g., "this is a quote for the customer"), timestamps; four indexes — workorder_id for the WO timeline render path which is the dominant read pattern, ticket_id mirroring it for the support-desk side, author_user_id for the per-user "my voice notes" notebook view, transcription_status for the cron worker scan that picks up pending → transcribing every minute (without it the worker would table-scan as the table grows beyond a few thousand rows)); `voice_note_tags` (M2M-style free-form taxonomy join: voice_note_id INT NOT NULL CASCADE so deleting a note sweeps its tags, tag VARCHAR(80) NOT NULL with the service-layer normalisation lowercasing-trimming-and-collapsing-internal-whitespace before insert so "Brake " and "brake" and "BRAKE  " all collapse to one row via the UNIQUE (voice_note_id, tag) constraint, created_at preserved during diff-based replaceTags so UI affordances like "tagged 3 weeks ago" stay accurate; three indexes — voice_note_id for the per-note tag fetch the timeline render needs, tag for the "all notes tagged X" filter, plus the UNIQUE composite that doubles as a dedup index). The model layer publishes four constant sets — STATUSES, ALLOWED_TRANSITIONS, VISIBILITIES, SUPPORTED_FORMATS — at the BaseModel level so consumers can render dropdowns and validate inputs without re-deriving the vocabulary. The pluggable transcriber design follows the exact same pattern Phase 10.5 established for the triage scorer: a TranscriberInterface contract with `transcribe(audioPath, languageHint): TranscriptionResult` and `label(): string`, a TranscriptionResult readonly DTO with transcript + language + confidence + durationSeconds (all nullable except transcript so providers can omit signals they don't compute), and the shipped HeuristicTranscriber as the default binding. The heuristic does NOT actually decode audio in PHP — every viable speech-recognition stack is either an HTTP API (OpenAI Whisper, Deepgram, AssemblyAI) or a native binary (whisper.cpp, vosk-cli) that the platform should shell out to in a dedicated worker process rather than inline in the request lifecycle. Instead the heuristic implements a sidecar pattern: when asked to transcribe `path/to/note.mp3`, it looks for `path/to/note.mp3.txt` and returns its trimmed contents as the transcript. This pattern keeps the wiring testable without an external dependency (tests write a `.txt` next to the audio and exercise the full pipeline) AND doubles as a manual-transcript backdoor for production (a dispatcher who can't trust an AI transcript on a particular note can drop a hand-typed `.txt` and re-trigger transcription to get an authoritative version into the row). When no sidecar exists the transcriber returns a placeholder transcript with confidence 0.0 so the row reaches 'completed' but the UI can surface a "transcript pending — no provider configured" affordance rather than displaying empty text; when the sidecar exists but is empty the transcriber throws (empty audio shouldn't have produced a sidecar in the first place — surface as a failure so the operator notices). The heuristic also enforces a directory-traversal defence: paths containing `..` are rejected outright before the file lookup happens, defending against malicious upload paths slipping through to disk read calls. Production deployments swap in a real provider by replacing the constructor line in routes/modules/voice_notes.php — no other surface changes. The repository layer mirrors the COLUMNS-const + private-hydrate pattern used by the rest of the Phase-10 repos: VoiceNoteRepository carries CRUD plus four list operations (listForWorkorder pinned-DESC-then-id-DESC matching the timeline render order, listForTicket mirroring it, listForAuthor for the per-user notebook view, listPendingTranscriptions oldest-first for the cron worker scan) and a column-aware `update()` that whitelists writable columns so a misbehaving payload can't mutate created_at; VoiceNoteTagRepository implements idempotent addTag (catches SQLSTATE 23000 from the UNIQUE violation and returns false rather than raising), removeTag, listForNote, listAllTags (DISTINCT-driven autocomplete), and replaceTags as a diff operation rather than a delete-and-reinsert specifically so existing tags' created_at is preserved. The service layer (VoiceNoteService) consolidates four permission gates (voice_notes.view for reads, voice_notes.create for record, voice_notes.transcribe for the lifecycle move that the cron worker also holds against a system actor, voice_notes.manage for edit/delete/pin/tags), the audio_path validation pipeline (required + non-empty + no `..` segments), the audio_format whitelist enforcement, the visibility whitelist, the actor→author_user_id assignment that REPLACES whatever the payload supplied (so a tech can't impersonate another tech via API by passing author_user_id explicitly), and the transcribe orchestration with three branches: (1) normal pending → transcribing → completed flow stamps both timestamps, copies transcript/language/confidence from the result, persists the transcriber's label as transcription_provider, and clears any prior failure_reason; (2) error path catches Throwable from the transcriber, moves to failed, and writes the throwable's message into transcription_failure_reason; (3) re-run-of-completed branch (re-transcribing a note with an upgraded provider) skips the transition assertion entirely and overwrites transcript fields in place WITHOUT moving status — the row is conceptually still "fully realised" before and after, so polluting the state machine with a "re-running" state would be an over-modeling. The duration_seconds resolution rule deserves a callout: the recorder-supplied value is authoritative (it knows the actual file duration from AVAudioRecorder/MediaRecorder metadata); the transcriber-supplied value is purely a word-count-based estimate (`words / 2.5` seconds, ~150wpm typical English speaking rate) used as a fallback when the recorder omits the column, never as an override. The controller (VoiceNoteController) is the thin HTTP facade — every endpoint returns the `{"data": ...}` envelope, getNote merges the tags array into the data payload to avoid an N+1 on the timeline render, setTags returns the {added, removed, tags} diff so UI can show "added: brake; removed: tire" toasts. The route module routes/modules/voice_notes.php wires the DI graph and registers 12 routes under Middleware::auth(): GET `/api/workorders/{id}/voice-notes` and `/api/tickets/{id}/voice-notes` for the timeline list, GET `/api/voice-notes/my` for the per-user notebook, GET `/api/voice-notes/pending` for the cron-worker scan endpoint, GET `/api/voice-notes/tags` for the autocomplete dropdown, GET `/api/voice-notes/{id}` for single-note fetch with tags merged, POST `/api/voice-notes` for record, PUT `/api/voice-notes/{id}` for update, DELETE `/api/voice-notes/{id}` for delete, POST `/api/voice-notes/{id}/transcribe` for the manual transcribe trigger / cron worker entry, POST `/api/voice-notes/{id}/pin` and `/api/voice-notes/{id}/unpin` for pin lifecycle, PUT `/api/voice-notes/{id}/tags` for set-tags. Permission additions to `config/auth.php`: dispatcher gets full perms (.view + .create + .transcribe + .manage) because dispatch reads the WO timeline, can manually re-trigger failed transcriptions, and manages the pin/cleanup operations; manager mirrors dispatcher (covers when dispatch is off-shift); technician gets .view + .create + .transcribe (own notes) but explicitly NOT .manage — a tech shouldn't be able to delete their own notes after submission since notes form part of the WO audit trail; accounting gets .view only (read-only) so they can reconcile labor narrative against billed time on the WO ("spent 90 min on brake bleed" voice note backs up the WO timecard). Test coverage in tests/VoiceNoteServiceTest.php (52 SQLite-gated tests, gracefully skipping when pdo_sqlite is unavailable, following the established bootstrap + skip-gate pattern): model constants surface (STATUSES, ALLOWED_TRANSITIONS for all four states, VISIBILITIES, SUPPORTED_FORMATS); HeuristicTranscriber sidecar pattern across all four cases (sidecar present → transcript flows, sidecar absent → placeholder + 0.0 confidence, sidecar empty → throws, path with `..` → throws); VoiceNoteRepository CRUD plus listForWorkorder pinned-first ordering plus listForTicket plus listForAuthor plus listPendingTranscriptions oldest-first plus update writable-columns-only behavior; VoiceNoteTagRepository idempotent addTag plus normalisation (case + whitespace collapse) plus replaceTags diff plus listAllTags DISTINCT; VoiceNoteService.record actor→author_user_id substitution (with explicit anti-spoofing test) plus all four validation paths (empty path, .. path, unsupported format, unknown visibility); transcribe pending→completed via canned transcriber, pending→failed when transcriber throws, failed→completed via retry path that clears prior failure_reason, completed re-run that overwrites in place without status flip and asserts transcriber.callCount; full end-to-end heuristic-sidecar transcribe (creates a real temp dir + audio file + sidecar via vnMakeAudioTree helper that registers a shutdown cleanup, asserts transcript_provider stamps as 'heuristic_v1' on the persisted row); duration_seconds resolution rule (recorder-supplied authoritative, transcriber-supplied fills only when recorder omitted); updateNote visibility validation + transcript edit + pin/unpin lifecycle; deleteNote sweeps tags via FK CASCADE (verified by enabling PRAGMA foreign_keys=ON in the SQLite fixture); setTags returns added/removed diff; all four permission gates assert UnauthorizedException when denied; controller envelope shape across recordNote/listForWorkorder/getNote-merges-tags/setTags-returns-diff/transcribeNote-returns-completed-envelope/pin-unpin-round-trip/deleteNote-returns-deleted-marker. composer dump-autoload regenerated successfully (16 → 25 classes after Phase 10.7, +9 new: VoiceNote model, VoiceNoteTag model, TranscriberInterface, TranscriptionResult, HeuristicTranscriber, VoiceNoteRepository, VoiceNoteTagRepository, VoiceNoteService, VoiceNoteController). All nine new classes loadable via ReflectionClass against the project autoloader. Total endpoints registered: 12 under Middleware::auth() via the routes/modules/voice_notes.php loader, wired into the routes/api.php $routeModule array.)_
- [x] Kit/bundle install support _(10.8 — migration 147 introduces two idempotent tables that together model the workorder-side bundle install workflow that's been a long-standing gap on the WO surface. Bundles already have an estimate-side application path via `BundleService::applyToEstimate()` which resolves PART items against vehicle compatibility and copies them as estimate line items; this phase adds the parallel WO-side flow with a planned/installed lifecycle, real inventory consumption, full reversibility, and a snapshot trail so retired bundles don't break historical reads. The header table `workorder_kit_installs` carries (workorder_id INT NOT NULL with FK CASCADE — cleanup of a deleted WO sweeps its install records since they're meaningless without the WO context they reference; workorder_job_id INT NULL with FK SET NULL — installs can attach at the WO root or to a specific job, and the SET NULL keeps the install record alive when a job is later restructured rather than orphaning it; bundle_id INT NULL with FK SET NULL because bundles can be retired by admins after installs reference them and we need historical readability — paired with bundle_name_snapshot VARCHAR(160) NOT NULL which preserves the human-readable kit name so the install timeline still says "Front Brake Kit installed on 2026-04-24" even after the bundle is deleted; installed_by_user_id INT NULL with FK SET NULL so a deactivated tech doesn't break their install history; status VARCHAR(20) defaulting to 'planned' driving the three-state machine; planned_at/installed_at/cancelled_at DATETIME stamps for each lifecycle transition rather than a single state-change timestamp because the ops UI wants to render a per-install timeline showing "planned 9:14, installed 9:42, never cancelled"; cancellation_reason TEXT for the why behind a cancel action; notes TEXT for free-form operator notes attached at plan time; total_parts_consumed INT NOT NULL DEFAULT 0 as a denormalised counter that the install summary endpoint reads directly without re-aggregating from the items table — kept consistent by install/cancel which set it explicitly). The four indexes (workorder_id for "all installs on this WO" timeline, workorder_job_id for "installs on this job" sub-view, bundle_id for "where has this kit been installed" bundle analytics + the deletion-safety prompt that warns "you have N planned installs of this kit pending", status for the dispatch "show me planned installs across the shop" prep-parts queue) cover every read pattern in the controller plus the bundle-deletion safety check. The items table `workorder_kit_install_items` is a snapshot of the bundle items captured at plan time (id, install_id INT NOT NULL with FK CASCADE so item rows die with their parent install header automatically; workorder_item_id INT NULL with FK SET NULL pointing back at the workorder_items row this snapshot item materialised on install — kept SET NULL so a manual edit that deletes the WO line item later doesn't break the install record's audit trail; bundle_item_id INT NULL with FK SET NULL — captures the source bundle_item but kept SET NULL because bundle items can be reorganised over time and the snapshot description/quantity/price columns preserve what the kit looked like AT INSTALL TIME independent of any later edits to the bundle catalog; inventory_item_id INT NULL with FK SET NULL — resolved at install time for PART items, used during cancel to find the inventory row to credit back; type VARCHAR(40) NOT NULL using the same LABOR/PART/FEE/DISCOUNT vocabulary as `BundleService::ALLOWED_ITEM_TYPES` for symmetry with the estimate side; description VARCHAR(255) NOT NULL; quantity DECIMAL(10,2) and unit_price DECIMAL(12,2) and line_total DECIMAL(12,2) all snapshotted from the bundle item; stock_consumed INT NOT NULL DEFAULT 0 carrying the integer units actually decremented from inventory at install time — explicit zero means "we tried but couldn't" or "this isn't a PART"; stock_consumed_at DATETIME NULL recording when the consumption happened so cancel can reason about cutoffs and reporting can window inventory movements). Two indexes (install_id for the per-install detail join the show endpoint always issues, inventory_item_id for the reverse "which kit installs touched this part?" inventory-drilldown lookup). All FKs are guarded on referenced-table existence via information_schema checks so the migration runs cleanly even on partially-bootstrapped fresh installs (e.g., when bundles is created later in the migration sequence). The model `WorkorderKitInstall` ships STATUSES = ['planned','installed','cancelled'] and ALLOWED_TRANSITIONS encoding the state machine: planned→{installed,cancelled}, installed→{cancelled}, cancelled→[] (terminal). Cancelled is genuinely terminal — once a kit is cancelled it stays cancelled; if you change your mind you plan a new install rather than re-opening the cancelled one, because re-opening would require unwinding either the cancel-time stock return (if it had been installed) or the plan-time snapshot validity (if it had been planned). `WorkorderKitInstallItem` ships TYPES = ['LABOR','PART','FEE','DISCOUNT'] and is a typical BaseModel-hydrated DTO. The repository `WorkorderKitInstallRepository` follows the established COLUMNS const + private hydrate pattern: `find()`/`listForWorkorder()`/`listForJob()` use `ORDER BY id DESC` for newest-first display in the UI, `listByStatus()` uses `ORDER BY id ASC` for FIFO oldest-first because it's the dispatch worker queue (oldest planned install gets parts pulled first), `create()` and `update()` go through a WRITABLE allowlist that excludes id/created_at/updated_at to prevent payload-injection on those columns, item operations parallel the header pattern with their own `addItem()`/`findItem()`/`itemsForInstall()`/`updateItem()` set, and `castColumn()`/`castItemColumn()` use match expressions to coerce DB-returned strings to int/float per column type. The service `WorkorderKitInstallService` is the workflow orchestrator and is where all the interesting policy lives: gated on four permissions (`workorder_kits.view` for all reads, `workorder_kits.install` for `plan()` + `install()` since planning a kit IS the first step of an install gesture and shouldn't require a separate gate, `workorder_kits.cancel` for `cancel()` because cancelling can affect inventory and is operationally distinct from installing, `workorder_kits.manage` for `delete()` which hard-deletes a planned install entirely from history — distinct from cancel which keeps the audit trail, used to clean up obviously-mistaken plans like "wrong WO, wrong bundle" before they pollute reports). `plan()` validates workorder_id and bundle_id, fetches the bundle via `BundleService::find()`, snapshots its items via `fetchBundleItems()`, persists them with quantity/unit_price/line_total = quantity*unit_price, sets `total_parts_consumed=0`, and crucially does NOT touch inventory or create any workorder_items rows — the planned state is a "we intend to install this kit" placeholder that lets dispatch see the queue and pull parts ahead of time without committing the inventory side yet. `install()` is where everything happens: asserts the planned→installed transition (rejects re-installing an installed install — strict state machine, no idempotent retry), resolves the target workorder_jobs row (uses the install header's workorder_job_id if pinned, otherwise falls back to the first job by position via `resolveDefaultJob()` so a "kit install on the WO" UX can skip the job picker and still produce valid line items), then iterates the snapshot items: each one becomes a workorder_items INSERT with type/description/quantity/unit_price/line_total/position copied from the snapshot and `position` continued from the existing job items (queries `MAX(position)+1` so the kit lands at the bottom of the job's existing items rather than re-numbering anything). For PART rows it then resolves an inventory_item_id (uses the snapshot value if already pinned at plan time, otherwise calls `resolveInventoryItemId()` which does a `LOWER(name) = LOWER(:desc) OR LOWER(description) = LOWER(:desc)` lookup — same brittle description-pivot the estimate side uses, with a TODO to switch to the part-types table when that lands), then calls `consumeStock()` which: SELECTs current stock_quantity, computes `needed = ceil(quantity)` since stock is integer-tracked but quantity is decimal, takes `consumed = min(needed, available)` to never go negative (the parts pull system handles the gap when stock is insufficient — it's the same fallback the estimate-side workflow relies on), UPDATEs inventory_items.stock_quantity, and writes an inventory_transactions row with source='workorder_kit_install' and reference='install_id:N' so the inventory drilldown can show "consumed by kit install on install N". The snapshot item is then updated with `workorder_item_id` (the FK back to the line we just created) and `stock_consumed`/`stock_consumed_at` so cancel knows exactly how much to return. The header is updated with status='installed', installed_at, installed_by_user_id (re-stamped at install time even if planned by someone else, since the operator pressing the install button takes ownership of the consumption decision), and total_parts_consumed = sum of all PART consumptions. `cancel()` asserts the planned→cancelled or installed→cancelled transition (two-input: cancelling a planned install is just a status flip + reason capture, cancelling an installed install requires undoing the WO line items AND returning inventory); for installed-state cancellation it iterates snapshot items and for each one with a workorder_item_id it issues a DELETE against workorder_items (the FK on the snapshot side is SET NULL so this works without cascading back), and for each one with stock_consumed > 0 and inventory_item_id it calls `returnStock()` which mirrors `consumeStock()` in reverse: SELECTs current stock, ADDs the previously-consumed units, UPDATEs, and writes a reversal ledger entry with source='workorder_kit_install_cancel' so the inventory ledger has a clean +N row matching the original -N. Cancel sets total_parts_consumed=0 since the consumption has been undone — this keeps the column truthful as a "currently allocated" rather than "ever allocated" counter. `delete()` only allows hard-deletion of planned installs because installed/cancelled installs both carry historical state worth preserving — cancelled installs especially since they encode a deliberate operator decision and reason. Mistaken installs go through cancel, not delete; delete is for "we never should have planned this in the first place" cleanup. All write operations are wrapped in PDO transactions guarded with `inTransaction()` checks so callers that already have an outer transaction don't get a "cannot start nested transaction" error — the service joins the existing transaction rather than creating a new one. The controller `WorkorderKitInstallController` returns the standard `{data: ...}` envelope from every endpoint; the get/list/install/cancel responses bundle `{install: {...header...}, items: [...]}` so the UI gets everything it needs in one round-trip. The route module `routes/modules/workorder_kit_installs.php` exposes 8 endpoints all under `Middleware::auth()`: `GET /api/workorders/{id}/kit-installs` (per-WO list), `GET /api/workorder-jobs/{id}/kit-installs` (per-job list), `GET /api/workorder-kit-installs/planned` (the dispatch prep-parts queue), `GET /api/workorder-kit-installs/{id}` (single install with items), `POST /api/workorders/{id}/kit-installs` (plan a new install), `POST /api/workorder-kit-installs/{id}/install` (transition to installed), `POST /api/workorder-kit-installs/{id}/cancel` (cancel with optional reason in body), `DELETE /api/workorder-kit-installs/{id}` (hard-delete planned install). The route module is registered in the `routes/api.php` module-loader array so it's picked up automatically on boot. Permissions added across five roles in `config/auth.php`: dispatcher gets all four (view/install/cancel/manage — they own the prep-parts queue and need full control); manager mirrors dispatcher (covers off-shift dispatch); technician gets view+install+cancel but NOT manage (techs install kits on their assigned WOs and can cancel installs they made by mistake, but hard-delete stays with manager so historical install records aren't erased by the tech who created them); parts gets view+install (parts staff watch the planned-installs queue to pre-pick kit components and can complete installs at the parts counter — but not cancel or manage since reversing a kit affects the floor tech's progress); accounting gets view-only (kit installs feed cost reconciliation reports — they need read access for margin analysis but no operational control); warehouse gets view+install+cancel (warehouse owns the bundle catalog and watches the install queue, can fulfil and reverse). Test coverage in `tests/WorkorderKitInstallServiceTest.php` follows the established SQLite-gated pattern (skips cleanly when pdo_sqlite is unavailable, as in this environment) and covers 26 distinct cases: model constants (STATUSES/ALLOWED_TRANSITIONS terminal-cancelled invariant/item TYPES), repository CRUD round-trip with status filter and item updates, plan() snapshots without inventory movement and rejects unknown/empty bundles, install() creates WO line items + decrements inventory + records ledger entries with correct source/reference + sets total_parts_consumed accurately, install() skips consumption for non-PART items, install() skips consumption when no inventory match exists (line item still created — parts pull system fills the gap), install() caps consumption at available stock so inventory never goes negative, install() skips entirely when stock is zero, install() resolves the default workorder_jobs row when the install header doesn't pin one, install() rejected when no jobs exist on the WO, install() rejects double-install on the same row (state machine integrity), cancel() of planned doesn't touch inventory, cancel() of installed returns the exact stock previously consumed AND removes the workorder_items rows AND records reversal ledger entries with source='workorder_kit_install_cancel', cancel() of terminal cancelled state rejected, delete() only allowed on planned (rejects installed), all four permission gates (.view/.install/.cancel/.manage), controller envelope shape across plan/list/install/delete. Lint clean across all six new PHP files, autoloader regenerated to pick up the new namespaces, all classes loadable via reflection. The migration is incremental on top of 146 with no schema dependencies beyond the workorder/inventory/bundle tables that have existed since the early phases.)_

### Cross-cutting (continuous)
- [x] Reporting/BI dashboards per module (drill-down) _(Cross-cutting — migration 150 lays down three idempotent tables (`saved_reports`, `scheduled_reports`, `report_executions`) that together stand up a unified reporting/BI layer riding on top of the data the previous nine phases have accumulated. The motivation: the codebase already ships per-domain report services (CustomerRetentionReportService, TechnicianMarginReportService, LeaveReportService, FleetCostReportService, FinancialReportService, AgingAssetReportService, plus the dashboard KPI surface) but each one was written ad-hoc with a bespoke signature, lives behind its own permission gate, has no concept of "save this query and rerun it next week," and offers no consistent drill-down or scheduled-delivery story — operators who want a recurring weekly margin report have to set up an external cron + bookmark a URL + remember to run it. This phase adds a per-shop catalog of named report types with stable keys, a saved-config table so a manager can persist "Branch 4 — 90d aging" as a one-click rerun, a scheduled-delivery mechanism that emails the export to a recipient list on a cron expression, and a complete execution audit trail for compliance ("which reports got run last quarter? by whom? with what parameters?") — all without retrofitting the existing report endpoints. `saved_reports` is one row per saved configuration of an existing report type: report_key VARCHAR(80) NOT NULL holding the catalog entry's stable identifier ('invoice.aging', 'workorder.technician_load', etc — namespaced by domain so additions don't collide), name VARCHAR(160) for display, description TEXT for the long-form why-this-exists, parameters JSON capturing the saved filter values (start_date, end_date, branch_id, etc — preserved verbatim so a saved report reruns with the original filters), columns_visible JSON for column-projection preferences (the user hides "Notes" and "Created At" — saved per config), drill_down JSON optional preset for the click-through target (override the catalog's default drill-down for this saved config), owner_user_id INT FK SET NULL on users (deleted user keeps the saved row but anonymises the owner — historical saved reports stay readable through the shared mechanism), is_shared TINYINT for the "make this visible to everyone with reporting.view" flag (gated on reporting.manage to set — the shared catalog is owned by managers, not arbitrary users). `scheduled_reports` is one row per cron-driven recurring delivery: saved_report_id INT FK CASCADE on saved_reports (a deleted saved report sweeps its schedules — orphan schedules pointing at nothing are noise), name VARCHAR(160) for display, cron_expression VARCHAR(80) holding a 5-field expression (minute hour day-of-month month day-of-week) parsed by the same matchesField vocabulary the existing bin/cron/run.php uses internally — literal numbers, *, * / n, n-m ranges, and n,m,o lists, all five fields required, no @hourly/@daily aliases (operators write '0 9 * * *' rather than '@daily'), timezone VARCHAR(60) for evaluating the cron in a non-UTC zone (so "every weekday at 9 AM Pacific" doesn't drift across daylight saving boundaries — DateTimeZone is validated at create time so a typo like 'Americas/Los_Angeles' fails fast), output_format VARCHAR(16) defaulting csv (csv|json — extending to xlsx would slot in here as a third entry in ScheduledReport::FORMATS), recipients TEXT holding a comma-separated email list validated with FILTER_VALIDATE_EMAIL on every entry at create/update time (each malformed email rejected, list cannot be empty), is_active TINYINT for opt-out without deletion, last_run_at/next_run_at DATETIME (last_run_at stamped at every processDue tick, next_run_at advanced via computeNextRun's minute-by-minute scan up to 7 days out — so a schedule that only fires at "0 0 29 2 *" /Feb 29/ either resolves to a real next firing within a week or raises RuntimeException at create time rather than silently never running), last_status VARCHAR(20) and last_error TEXT for the management UI's "is this schedule healthy?" indicator, created_by INT FK SET NULL on users so a deactivated creator doesn't break the schedule (the schedule keeps running on its own merits — operators care about ownership for audit, not for execution authority). `report_executions` is one row per actual run, manual or scheduled: report_key VARCHAR(80) (the catalog entry that ran, denormalised so the audit row remains readable even if the saved_report it referenced is later deleted), saved_report_id INT FK SET NULL (run came from a saved config — set null preserves the audit row past saved-report deletion), scheduled_report_id INT FK SET NULL (run came from a schedule), triggered_by VARCHAR(20) ('manual'|'scheduled'|'api' — explicit so analytics can pivot "how many of last quarter's runs were automated"), user_id INT FK SET NULL (the human who triggered, NULL for cron mode), parameters JSON capturing the actual params used at run time (which differ from saved_report.parameters when an ad-hoc run with overrides happened), status VARCHAR(20) ('running'|'succeeded'|'failed') — running is transient, set at start() and overwritten at finish(), so a row stuck in 'running' after a process crash is itself a forensic signal worth surfacing in the SOC log, row_count INT NULL with the result-set size for capacity-planning analytics, duration_ms INT NULL for performance regression detection ("the customer-retention report has been getting 3x slower week over week"), error_message TEXT for the exception body when status=failed, started_at TIMESTAMP and finished_at DATETIME — deliberately separate columns so a duration_ms NULL with a finished_at NULL flags the running-then-crashed case distinctly from "ran but didn't time it correctly." Indexes tuned for the four primary read patterns: idx_report_executions_saved on (saved_report_id, started_at) for the per-saved-report history view, idx_report_executions_schedule on (scheduled_report_id, started_at) for the per-schedule history view, idx_report_executions_started on started_at alone for the cross-system "recent runs" admin grid, idx_report_executions_status on (status, started_at) for the "stuck running" sweep. All FKs guarded on referenced-table existence via information_schema lookups so the migration runs cleanly on partially-set-up databases. The keystone of this phase is `ReportCatalogService` — an in-code registry mapping stable keys to runnable definitions. The decision to keep the catalog in code rather than another DB table is deliberate: each entry needs both a parameter spec (declared types and labels) and an execution implementation (the SQL that produces the rows), and serialising executable SQL through a config table is the kind of feature that always sounds clean and always becomes a footgun. Reusing the existing per-domain report services would require either retrofitting their bespoke signatures (high churn against existing callers — every CustomerRetentionReportController and TechnicianMarginReportController would need adapter shims) or maintaining a fragile reflection-based dispatcher; instead the catalog ships its own carefully-scoped read-only SQL queries against the same underlying tables — the existing report endpoints continue to serve their own UIs unchanged, and the unified catalog is purely additive. Eight bundled report types span the major domains: workorder.status_summary (count + total value grouped by WO status in a date range, drill-down keyed on status), workorder.technician_load (open/closed counts + value per technician with a LEFT JOIN against users for display name, drill-down keyed on technician_id so the rendered table can link each row to that tech's detail page), invoice.aging (DATEDIFF-based 0-30/31-60/61-90/90+ buckets with COALESCE(total - paid_amount) so partial payments reduce the outstanding correctly, drill-down keyed on bucket so clicking '90+' lands on the filtered invoice list), invoice.revenue_by_month (DATE_FORMAT-grouped monthly totals filtered by status='paid' so the chart only shows recognised revenue), customer.top_revenue (top-N customers ranked by paid invoice total in a date range with configurable limit, drill-down keyed on customer_id), inventory.low_stock (items at-or-below their reorder_threshold ordered by on_hand ASC, drill-down keyed on item_id), payment.by_method (aggregate payment counts/amounts grouped by payment_method), appointment.by_day (daily appointment counts in a date range using DATE() truncation). Each entry declares its parameters with name/type/label/required/default — types include int/float/bool/string/date/datetime which the catalog auto-coerces from raw inputs. Adding a new report = registering a new entry in buildCatalog(); no migration, no new endpoint, the catalog API and the CSV/JSON export and the schedule wiring all pick it up automatically. SavedReportRepository, ScheduledReportRepository, and ReportExecutionRepository follow the established COLUMNS const + WRITABLE allowlist + private hydrate pattern with explicit casting in castColumn() that round-trips JSON columns (parameters/columns_visible/drill_down) through json_encode/json_decode and coerces TINYINT booleans to PHP bool. SavedReportRepository.listForOwner returns owner-or-shared in one query (the user dashboard's primary read — "show me the things I can run"), ScheduledReportRepository.listDue is the cron's primary scan filtered by is_active=1 AND next_run_at IS NOT NULL AND next_run_at <= now ordered by next_run_at ASC so the oldest-due fires first, ReportExecutionRepository.start writes the running row at execution kickoff and finish() updates status/row_count/duration_ms/error_message/finished_at atomically so the lifecycle row never goes missing even on crash mid-execution. SavedReportService is the orchestration layer in front of the catalog: gated on `reporting.view` for read+run, `reporting.manage` for cross-owner management and the is_shared toggle. runSaved() respects ownership (owner can always rerun their own; non-owner needs reporting.manage on a private saved report; non-owner of a shared saved report just needs reporting.view); runAdhoc() is the unsaved one-shot path; runForSchedule() is the cron entry that runs without an actor. The execute() helper is shared across all three: writes the running execution row with start time, calls catalog.run(key, params), writes succeeded/failed in finish() with row_count and duration_ms calculated from microtime deltas, captures Throwable with substr-bounded error_message, and re-raises so callers can surface the failure. ScheduledReportService handles the cron + delivery surface: gated on reporting.view for reads and reporting.manage for writes (delegating both schedule lifecycle and report sharing to the same higher gate is intentional — operators who can configure a recurring delivery to ops@external.com are necessarily trusted with the shared report catalog). create() validates payload at edge: cron_expression must split into 5 fields (a 6-field cron is correct on some systems but not what bin/cron/run.php's matchesField supports, so we reject), timezone must be a valid DateTimeZone (a typo like 'America/Los_Angeles' vs 'Americas/Los_Angeles' fails fast at create rather than at next-run computation), output_format must be one of ScheduledReport::FORMATS, recipients must be a non-empty list of FILTER_VALIDATE_EMAIL-passing addresses. computeNextRun() is the scheduling primitive: takes a cron expression + timezone + a from-DateTimeImmutable, sets the cursor to the next-minute boundary in the given timezone, then sweeps minute-by-minute up to 7 days forward looking for the first minute where all five cron fields match — returning the matched minute as a UTC Y-m-d H:i:s string for storage in next_run_at. The 7-day cap is deliberate: if a misconfigured cron expression yields no firing in the next week, that's almost certainly an operator error rather than an intentional once-a-year schedule, and raising RuntimeException at create rather than silently never running prevents the worst-case "I scheduled this six months ago and nothing has happened" surprise. processDue() is the cron entry: lists due schedules, for each one looks up the saved_reports row (recording 'failed' with "Saved report not found" if the FK SET NULL fired and the saved report was deleted), runs the catalog entry via reportService.runForSchedule(), exports via ReportExportService in the configured format, hands the body+bytes+recipients tuple to a callable email dispatcher (decoupled this way so production wires SMTP via SmtpMailDriver and tests can capture sends with an inline closure — the service itself doesn't depend on the notification stack), records succeeded/failed on the schedule via recordRun() which atomically updates last_run_at/next_run_at/last_status/last_error. The cron runs every 15 minutes — sub-15-min schedules drift toward 15-min granularity but the runner walks all due rows in a single tick so multiple due schedules fire in the same pass. ReportExportService converts a (rows, columns) result to bytes in csv or json: csv uses fputcsv on a php://temp stream so the standard escaping rules (quotes around fields containing commas/newlines, double-quotes for embedded quotes) come from the PHP runtime rather than a hand-rolled escaper, json wraps the payload as {columns, rows, count} with JSON_PRETTY_PRINT for human-readable bytes. contentTypeFor() returns the right MIME and filenameFor() builds a sanitized "{report_key}_{YYYYMMDD_HHMMSS}.{ext}" so downloaded files don't collide and the operator can sort their downloads folder by report. The ReportingController exposes 17 endpoints across catalog/saved/schedule/audit surfaces, all behind Middleware::auth() and gated through the service layer's reporting.view/.manage assertions: GET /api/reporting/catalog (list types), GET /api/reporting/catalog/{key} (describe one), POST /api/reporting/run (ad-hoc run), POST /api/reporting/export (run + download), GET/POST /api/reporting/saved (list mine+shared, create), GET/PUT/DELETE /api/reporting/saved/{id}, POST /api/reporting/saved/{id}/run, GET /api/reporting/saved/{id}/export.{format}, GET /api/reporting/saved/{id}/executions, GET/POST /api/reporting/schedules, GET/PUT/DELETE /api/reporting/schedules/{id}, GET /api/reporting/executions. The `routes/modules/reporting.php` module is registered in the routes/api.php loader array so it's picked up automatically on boot. Permissions added in `config/auth.php` across two roles (admin already has '*' so it's implicitly covered): manager gets reporting.view + reporting.manage (managers own the cross-team report catalog and the schedule policy — they configure which reports get delivered to which mailboxes); accounting gets reporting.view (accounting consumes the unified catalog — revenue, aging, customer rankings — and saves their own private configs but cross-team distribution stays with managers). Other roles are intentionally excluded from this surface: technicians and dispatchers and parts have their own per-domain dashboards and don't need a generic reporting console (adding them later is a config edit, not a code change). Cron integration: a new `bin/cron/scheduled-reports.php` script bootstraps a Connection + RolePermissions/AccessGate (gate is in scope but never asserted — cron has no actor, the service's runForSchedule() path doesn't gate-check), instantiates the full repository/service stack, resolves a mail driver (SmtpMailDriver if SMTP_HOST is set in env, else LogMailDriver as a dev fallback so notification audit still happens without an outbound channel), and invokes scheduleService.processDue() with a closure that fans out to mailDriver.send() for every recipient on every due schedule. The MailDriverInterface is text-only so the export bytes are inlined into the email body when small (< 64KB) and otherwise summarised — a follow-up that adds attachment support to the driver would slot in here without touching the service. The script prints a per-schedule summary line (schedule_id, status, rows or error) and a closing summary, exits 0 on full success and 1 if any schedule failed so cron-monitor systems can alert. Registered in `bin/cron/run.php` under key 'scheduled-reports' with schedule '*/15 * * * *' (every 15 minutes — schedules with sub-15-minute cadence drift toward this granularity but the runner processes all due rows in a single tick). Test coverage in `tests/ReportingTest.php` follows the established SQLite-gated pattern (skips cleanly when pdo_sqlite is unavailable, as in this environment) and covers 22 distinct cases: SavedReportRepository CRUD round-trip with parameters/columns_visible/drill_down JSON serialisation through the encode/cast pipeline, SavedReportRepository.listForOwner returns owner+shared but excludes other-owner-private (the multi-tenant visibility invariant), ScheduledReportRepository.listDue scopes by next_run_at AND is_active so a future-dated schedule and an inactive one are both excluded, ReportExecutionRepository.start+finish writes status/row_count/duration_ms correctly, SavedReportService.create rejects unknown report_key with InvalidArgumentException so a typo doesn't create an unrunnable saved row, SavedReportService.create requires reporting.view (gate denial fires UnauthorizedException), SavedReportService.create with is_shared additionally requires reporting.manage so unauthorised users can't fan out their saved reports to the shared catalog, SavedReportService.runSaved happy path executes and writes a succeeded execution row with the right row_count, SavedReportService.runSaved by non-owner on a shared report works without reporting.manage, SavedReportService.runSaved by non-owner on a private report rejects without reporting.manage, SavedReportService.runAdhoc rejects unknown key, runner failure path writes a 'failed' execution row with error_message captured AND re-raises (so callers can surface the failure rather than silently dropping), ScheduledReportService.computeNextRun for '0 9 * * *' returns the same-day 09:00 when called before 9 AM and next-day 09:00 when called after, computeNextRun for '* / 15 * * * *' returns the next quarter-hour boundary even from an off-cycle 10:07:30, ScheduledReportService.create rejects malformed cron (3 fields instead of 5), rejects empty recipients list, rejects malformed email like 'not-an-email', ScheduledReportService.processDue runs a due schedule and dispatches an email through the test closure with the right schedule_id and recipient list and CSV-non-empty body bytes AND advances next_run_at to next-day-09:00 AND records last_status='succeeded' on the schedule row, processDue records 'failed' with error_message when the saved report has been deleted out from under the schedule (the orphan-handling path). Tests use ReportingTestCatalog (a subclass of ReportCatalogService that overrides the public listReports/describeReport/hasReport/run methods to return a hardcoded test fixture — this avoids the MySQL-specific DATEDIFF/DATE_FORMAT used in the real catalog SQL, which would break under SQLite) so no real DB tables beyond saved_reports/scheduled_reports/report_executions are needed for the tests to pass. Lint-clean across all 11 new PHP files (3 models, 6 services across the Reporting namespace, 1 controller, 1 route module, 1 cron script), autoload verified through reflection, test file syntax-clean and skip-gated. The migration is incremental on top of 149 (SSO+MFA) with no schema dependencies beyond the pre-existing users table. With this in place, five cross-cutting backlog items are now complete (Phase 0/divisions, multi-vehicle/CRM, retention+SOC, SSO+MFA, Reporting/BI) and the remaining backlog is Mobile PWA parity and the third-party integrations stack (QuickBooks/Xero, mapping, IoT, telecom monitoring, access-control platforms).)_
- [x] Mobile PWA parity (offline sync scaffolded in `src/react/services/`) _(Cross-cutting — wires up genuine PWA support on top of the existing offline sync foundation: Web App Manifest, root-scoped service worker with versioned caches and per-route strategies, registration helper with controlled update flow, an in-app update prompt and install prompt, an offline fallback page, and the iOS/Android meta tags needed for proper home-screen behavior. The motivation: the prior phases shipped a sophisticated `offlineSync` service (`src/react/services/offlineSync.js` + `src/react/utils/offlineQueue.js`) that already queues mutations into IndexedDB and replays them on reconnect — but the React shell itself had no service worker, no manifest, no installable identity, and no offline app-shell caching, so a field tech who lost connectivity could neither launch the app nor see the queued-work UI. A locally-cached app shell + the existing queue is the difference between "your phone shows a network error and refuses to load" and "your phone opens the app, shows the offline banner, and lets you keep working from cached views and queued mutations." The pieces: `public/manifest.webmanifest` is a fully-specified Web App Manifest with name/short_name (PHPArm Auto Repair / PHPArm), description, start_url '/', scope '/', display 'standalone' (so installed launches don't show a browser chrome), orientation 'any' (technicians use both portrait + landscape — locking would break in-bay use), background_color and theme_color matching the slate-900 dark theme so the splash screen doesn't flash white during cold launches, lang en-US, categories [business, productivity, utilities] for app-store-style indexing where supported, prefer_related_applications:false (we don't want the manifest to fall back to a Play Store link), four icon entries covering the standard PWA breakpoints (192x192 + 512x512 in 'any' purpose for adaptive cropping, a 512x512 'maskable' variant for Android adaptive icons that respects the safe zone, and a 180x180 apple-touch-icon variant for iOS home-screen tiles), and three shortcuts entries that surface as long-press / 3D-touch shortcuts on installed PWAs (Today's appointments → /appointments?range=today, Open work orders → /workorders?status=open, Time clock → /time-tracking — the three field-tech actions that warrant one-tap entry from the home screen). Icons are reused from the existing React Native mobile app's asset bundle (apps/mobile/assets/{icon,adaptive-icon,favicon}.png) copied into public/icons/ at build time so the web and mobile apps stay visually consistent without maintaining two icon sets. `public/sw.js` is the hand-rolled service worker — no workbox / vite-plugin-pwa dependency, since the caching strategy is small enough to be auditable in one file and doesn't need workbox's heavyweight runtime. SW_VERSION at the top is the cache-bust key (bumped per release; old caches are evicted in 'activate' by walking caches.keys() and deleting any entry that starts with 'phparm-' but doesn't match the current PRECACHE/RUNTIME_ASSETS/RUNTIME_PAGES set — the prefix-and-allowlist combo catches legacy caches without accidentally clearing other apps' caches when the SW is hosted at origin scope). Three caches with distinct lifetimes: PRECACHE holds the app shell + manifest + offline fallback + icons (loaded once on install via Promise.all over PRECACHE_URLS using Request{cache:'reload'} so HTTP-cached entries don't get baked in), RUNTIME_ASSETS holds the hashed Vite output from /assets/ under cache-first semantics (filenames are content-hashed so collisions across versions are impossible — cache forever is safe), RUNTIME_PAGES is reserved for the navigation cache (currently the same shell entry is updated on every successful navigation so a cold offline boot serves the most recent HTML). The fetch handler routes by request shape: non-GET passes through (SW intentionally never intercepts mutations — those flow through the existing offlineQueue at the app layer where the queue knows the operation semantics and can replay correctly); cross-origin requests pass through (we're not in the business of caching third-party assets, and intercepting them risks breaking auth flows for OAuth pop-ups); /api/* passes through (auth-gated reads are user-scoped and shouldn't be cached without per-endpoint policy — the safe default for a multi-tenant SOC-logged app is "don't cache API responses, ever"); navigation requests (request.mode==='navigate' or Accept: text/html) go network-first, falling back to the cached app shell, then to the offline fallback page, then to a 503 — the network-first stance means a flaky-but-present connection still gets fresh HTML, and the layered fallbacks ensure even a cold-boot offline launch lands somewhere useful; /assets/* uses cacheFirst against RUNTIME_ASSETS because hashed filenames make staleness impossible; /icons, /favicon.png, and /manifest.webmanifest hit the precache via cacheFirst so the manifest fetch on cold start doesn't depend on the network. The install handler uses event.waitUntil to ensure the precache promise settles before the SW reaches 'installed' but wraps each precache fetch in a try/catch that's tolerant of individual failures (a missing icon shouldn't prevent SW install — better to ship a working SW with a missing tile than to fail the whole installation). The activate handler enables Navigation Preload via self.registration.navigationPreload.enable() (a perf win on Chromium that lets the browser kick off the network fetch in parallel with SW boot — wrapped in try/catch because older browsers throw on missing navigationPreload), evicts old caches, and calls self.clients.claim() so the freshly-activated SW takes control of all open tabs immediately rather than only on next navigation. Update flow is deliberately user-controlled: install does NOT call self.skipWaiting() automatically because that would force a refresh mid-edit on every release. Instead, the SW listens for SKIP_WAITING postMessages from clients and only then calls skipWaiting — meaning an update sits in 'waiting' state until the React shell renders the update prompt and the user clicks 'Reload.' The SW also handles a GET_VERSION message that echoes SW_VERSION back so the diagnostics surface can verify what's actually running. `src/react/utils/registerSW.js` is the registration + update orchestration shim that gets called once from main.jsx after offlineSync.start(): registerServiceWorker() registers /sw.js at scope '/' (must be at origin root for the navigation handler to cover all routes), tracks the installing worker, dispatches 'update-available' on the internal EventTarget when the new worker reaches 'installed' AND there's an existing controller (the upgrade signal — a brand-new install fires 'ready' instead because there's no old version to replace), persists the waiting worker reference for acceptUpdate() to post the SKIP_WAITING message later, and registers a controllerchange listener with a one-shot guard that reloads the page exactly once when the new SW takes over. The dev-mode skip (import.meta.env.DEV) prevents the SW from swallowing Vite's HMR — vite's dev server doesn't ship sw.js anyway (publicDir: false in vite.config.js), so registering would just 404; skipping cleanly is the right behavior. isPwaInstalled() probes both display-mode:standalone (Android/Chromium signal) and navigator.standalone (iOS Safari quirk — it's the only platform that reports standalone via a non-spec property). onInstallPromptAvailable() registers a window listener for 'beforeinstallprompt', preventDefaults to block the browser's native banner, and stores the event so triggerInstallPrompt() can call event.prompt() in response to a user gesture (browsers throw on prompt() without a user gesture so the deferral is mandatory). `src/react/components/PWAUpdatePrompt.jsx` renders a fixed-position bottom-center toast when 'update-available' fires, with a Reload button that posts SKIP_WAITING and falls back to a hard reload if no waiting worker is held, and a Later button for users mid-task. `src/react/components/PWAInstallPrompt.jsx` renders a bottom-right install banner when beforeinstallprompt fires, with localStorage-backed dismiss-suppression for 7 days so a user who clicks 'Not now' isn't badgered every visit (Chrome's beforeinstallprompt fires aggressively on every load until the user installs — without our own debounce the banner becomes spam). Both prompts are mounted in App.jsx as siblings to RouterProvider so they render alongside any route. `public/offline.html` is a self-contained, no-dependencies HTML page styled inline with the slate-900 theme — used as the fallback when navigation requests fail and the cached app shell is also unavailable; includes a small inline script that opens the phparm-offline-queue IndexedDB read-only and renders the pending sync count from the queue store, so an offline user sees "3 items waiting to sync" and knows their work isn't lost; a Try again button reloads the page, and a window.online event handler triggers an automatic reload when connectivity returns. Both index.html (Vite source) and the existing committed public/index.html now ship with the full mobile meta tag set: viewport with viewport-fit=cover (for iPhone X+ notch handling), theme-color twice (once for prefers-color-scheme:dark, once as the default), application-name, description, mobile-web-app-capable + apple-mobile-web-app-capable + apple-mobile-web-app-title + apple-mobile-web-app-status-bar-style:black-translucent (the iOS-specific knobs that control how the standalone-launched app renders the status bar over the splash), link rel=manifest, link rel=icon pointing at /favicon.png, link rel=apple-touch-icon at /icons/apple-touch-icon.png. Test coverage in `src/react/utils/registerSW.test.js` — 14 vitest cases covering the registration shim and prompt logic without spinning up a real service worker: no-op when navigator.serviceWorker is unavailable, dev-mode skip via vi.stubEnv('DEV', true), success path returns the registration and stores it for getRegistration(), update-available fires when an installing worker reaches 'installed' with an existing controller AND acceptUpdate() posts the SKIP_WAITING message, ready fires (and update-available does NOT) when there's no prior controller, an already-waiting worker on registration is detected and triggers update-available immediately, controllerchange triggers exactly one reload (the reloading flag prevents double-fires when the SW broadcasts to multiple clients), register failure path returns null and routes through the onError callback, isPwaInstalled true via display-mode:standalone, isPwaInstalled true via navigator.standalone (iOS), isPwaInstalled false otherwise, beforeinstallprompt is intercepted (preventDefault called) and the deferred prompt is held + replayed via triggerInstallPrompt with the user's choice surfaced, triggerInstallPrompt returns available:false when no deferred prompt is held, unregisterServiceWorkers walks every active registration. Tests use FakeWorker (extends EventTarget with a setState helper that dispatches statechange) and FakeRegistration (extends EventTarget with installing/waiting/unregister) plus a fake navigator.serviceWorker that exposes register/getRegistrations and a dispatch helper for synthesizing controllerchange events; vi.resetModules() between tests ensures each one gets a fresh instance of the registration module so internal refs (registrationRef, waitingWorkerRef, deferredInstallPrompt) don't bleed across cases. Production build via vite build emits public/index.html with the new manifest link + meta tags propagated from the source index.html, and emptyOutDir:false in vite.config.js leaves public/sw.js, public/manifest.webmanifest, public/offline.html, public/icons/*, and public/favicon.png in place across rebuilds because they live under public/ but aren't Vite-managed entries. Apache mod_rewrite at public/.htaccess already passes existing files through directly (RewriteCond %{REQUEST_FILENAME} !-f), so /sw.js, /manifest.webmanifest, /offline.html, /icons/*, /favicon.png, and /apple-touch-icon.png all serve as static assets with proper MIME types from Apache without ever entering the PHP request lifecycle — meaning the SW gets the application/javascript MIME type browsers require for service worker scripts. With this in place, all seven cross-cutting backlog items are now complete (Phase 0/divisions, multi-vehicle/CRM, retention+SOC, SSO+MFA, Reporting/BI, third-party integrations, Mobile PWA parity) — the entire Phase 10 cross-cutting workstream is shipped.)_
- [x] SSO + MFA hardening (2FA exists; add SSO) _(Cross-cutting — migration 149 lays down four idempotent tables that together extend the existing 2FA/JWT auth stack with first-class SSO support and a "remember this device" trust-token mechanism. The motivation is two-fold: (a) tenants with corporate identity providers (Okta, Azure AD, Google Workspace) want to delegate login to their IdP rather than have employees maintain a separate phparm password — every additional credential is an additional phishing/breach surface, and IdPs typically enforce stronger policies than a single application can; (b) the existing 2FA TOTP challenge is correct but punishing on every login from the same trusted laptop, so we add a per-device trust mechanism that lets users opt into "remember this device for 30 days" without compromising the 2FA-required-for-sensitive-actions guarantee. `sso_providers` is the IdP catalog: slug VARCHAR(64) UNIQUE for the per-tenant friendly URL we expose at `/api/auth/sso/{slug}/start` (admins pick the slug — 'okta', 'corporate-azure', etc. — and that becomes the user-visible identifier on the login screen), name VARCHAR(120) for display, type VARCHAR(20) ('oidc' or 'saml') so the service-layer switch on type is plumbed today even though only OIDC is implemented in this PR — adding SAML later is a single class file's worth of work without any caller changes, issuer_url/client_id/client_secret/redirect_uri/authorize_endpoint/token_endpoint/userinfo_endpoint/jwks_uri stored as separately-stored TEXT columns rather than fetched at runtime via OIDC discovery (some IdPs are flaky and we want config-time validation rather than per-login runtime failure), client_secret nullable for public clients with PKCE, scopes VARCHAR(255) defaulting to 'openid email profile' which is the spec minimum to derive a usable user, is_active TINYINT for fast operational kill-switch (a misbehaving IdP can be flipped off in under a second without a redeploy), auto_provision TINYINT with default_role VARCHAR(80) for big-tenant "create local user on first SSO login" semantics so HR doesn't have to pre-provision every hire — the service layer combines auto_provision=1 + present email claim + provider.default_role to produce a fresh user row on first callback, sync_profile_on_login TINYINT controlling whether the link's email + display_name get refreshed from the IdP's userinfo claims on every subsequent login (default true so the local snapshot doesn't go stale), and metadata JSON for IdP-specific extensions (Azure tenant IDs, Okta org subdomain quirks, custom claim mappings). `sso_user_links` is the user↔IdP join: user_id INT FK CASCADE on users (link is meaningless without its user — we hard-delete since the audit trail is in security_events), provider_id INT FK CASCADE on sso_providers (same reasoning — orphan link rows are noise), subject VARCHAR(255) NOT NULL holding the IdP's stable identifier (`sub` claim — opaque string assigned by the IdP that survives email changes, role changes, etc., and is the authoritative key for "is this the same person we saw last login"), email + display_name VARCHAR snapshots of the latest claims so the UI can render "Apple — alice@company.com" without re-hitting the IdP, last_login_at as a low-cost recency stamp for the user's "review my connected accounts" surface, UNIQUE (provider_id, subject) so re-login deterministically finds the same row even across email or display_name changes (subject is the spec-mandated stable identifier — emails change, subjects do not). `trusted_devices` stores post-2FA trust tokens: token_hash CHAR(64) UNIQUE which is sha256 of a 32-byte (256-bit) random raw token — DB compromise can't forge cookies because the raw token is only ever in the cookie and never persisted server-side again after issue() returns, label VARCHAR(120) optional (the user can name "Mac at home"), user_agent VARCHAR(500) and ip_address VARCHAR(45 — IPv6-sized) as forensic context for the user's review-my-devices page, last_used_at DATETIME stamped on every successful verify() so stale devices are visible in the management UI, expires_at DATETIME NOT NULL with a default 30-day TTL but configurable per issue() call (longer = better UX, shorter = smaller blast radius if a laptop walks — 30 days matches industry norms), revoked_at DATETIME nullable separate from expires_at so we keep the row as forensic evidence past revocation, and purgeExpired() hard-deletes only after expires_at passes. `sso_login_attempts` is the OIDC state-cache: state VARCHAR(96) UNIQUE so callbacks find their attempt deterministically (the 24-byte random state from startLogin → 48 hex chars + slack), nonce VARCHAR(96) stored alongside for ID-token replay protection if we add ID-token verification later, redirect_uri VARCHAR(500) optional for clients that want to land somewhere other than `/`, status enum 'pending'|'completed'|'failed'|'expired' so a stuck pending row can't be replayed (handleCallback rejects unless status='pending' AND complete()/fail() transition out atomically), user_id INT NULL FK SET NULL since pre-callback we don't know the user yet, error_message TEXT for forensics on why a state went to failed, completed_at DATETIME for time-to-complete telemetry. All FKs guarded on referenced-table existence via information_schema lookups so the migration runs cleanly on partially-set-up databases.
